Cybersecurity English

A cybersecurity English curriculum for incident response, vulnerability triage, identity, threat modeling, risk communication, compliance, executive briefings, and security pushback.

For security analysts, SOC staff, incident responders, security engineers, GRC specialists, identity teams, vulnerability managers, and security leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Give a concise alert handoff that separates logged events, plausible explanations, and unresolved authorization.
  • Coordinate a response update with action status, operational impact, evidence preservation, and a defined next report.
  • Explain a risk-based remediation order without treating severity scores as complete business-risk assessments.
  • Negotiate a specific access arrangement using identity, task, permission, duration, approval, and accountability.
  • Trace a data flow, identify a trust boundary, and turn a specific abuse case into a testable design requirement.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

Original fictional English practice, not a security certification, incident-response playbook, legal opinion, or authorization to test systems. Follow the responsible team, approved procedures, evidence-handling rules, and applicable requirements. All organizations, incidents, figures, priorities, and control criteria in the cases are invented.

01Security Triage and Alert InvestigationA successful login is not verified authorization+

Your goal

Give a concise alert handoff that separates logged events, plausible explanations, and unresolved authorization.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

A successful login is not verified authorization

Analyst Anika reviews alert S14 with shift lead Luis. The identity log records a successful privileged-account sign-in at 02:14 UTC from an unfamiliar address. A maintenance ticket covers 02:00-03:00 UTC, but its host and account have not been matched to the event. The account owner has not replied. Application logs are missing for 02:10-02:20 UTC. No malicious action is confirmed. The local process requires escalation to Luis now while correlation continues.

Anika: Security analyst / Luis: Shift lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the identity log establish?

Answer and explanation

C. A successful privileged-account sign-in at 02:14 UTC

The log establishes the sign-in event, not the identity or permission behind it.

2. What is missing from 02:10-02:20 UTC?

Answer and explanation

A. Application logs

The briefing identifies an application-log gap during the relevant period.

3. What does the local process require now?

Answer and explanation

B. Escalation to Luis while correlation continues

The stated process requires prompt escalation without first asserting a confirmed cause.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A successful authentication does not establish that the account holder intended or authorized the action. A matching time window supports a possible maintenance explanation, not a confirmed match to this event.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
alert
A notification that a detection condition was met.triage an alert
event
An observable occurrence recorded by a system.correlate events
incident
An occurrence assessed as compromising or threatening security.classify an incident
triage
Initial assessment to decide priority and next handling.perform security triage
privileged account
An identity with elevated system permissions.review privileged-account activity
successful sign-in
A recorded completion of an authentication process.verify a successful sign-in
authentication
Checking evidence that an identity claim is valid.examine authentication records
authorization
Permission for a particular action or resource.confirm authorization
source address
A network address associated with an observed connection.investigate a source address
timestamp
A recorded time associated with an event.preserve the timestamp
UTC
Coordinated Universal Time, a shared time reference.report times in UTC
maintenance window
A defined period for planned maintenance work.confirm the maintenance window
correlation
Relating records to assess whether events are connected.correlate identity and host logs
log source
A system or service producing event records.identify the log source
telemetry
System observations collected for monitoring or analysis.review available telemetry
visibility gap
A period or area lacking needed observation data.disclose a visibility gap
baseline
A reference pattern used to evaluate current behavior.compare activity with the baseline
anomaly
A departure from an expected pattern.investigate an anomaly
false positive
A detection that incorrectly indicates the target condition.validate a false positive
benign activity
Activity assessed as not harmful in its context.confirm benign activity
indicator
An observable sign that can support an investigation.assess an indicator
hypothesis
A proposed explanation awaiting evaluation.test the maintenance hypothesis
confidence level
A qualified assessment of evidential strength.state the confidence level
case record
A documented account of observations and handling.update the case record

No malicious action confirmed is not the same as no malicious action occurred. The missing application logs restrict what the team can exclude; keep that limitation in the handoff.

C Notice the language

Ask without accusing

A direct question about who authorized an action is a request for evidence, not necessarily an accusation. Use event details and neutral verbs. Neither familiarity with a colleague nor an urgent alert label settles whether a particular action was permitted.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the event
"The identity log records a successful sign-in at 02:14 UTC."
Identify the privilege
"The event involves a privileged account."
Separate the conclusion
"Authorization is not yet verified."
Name the plausible explanation
"The timing overlaps planned maintenance."
Limit the match
"We have not matched the host and account."
Identify missing evidence
"Application logs are missing for ten minutes."
Avoid an accusation
"Who can confirm the authorized operator?"
Preserve urgency
"I am escalating while correlation continues."
State uncertainty
"No malicious action is confirmed at this point."
Bound the search
"That conclusion applies only to the sources checked."
Use neutral wording
"The address is unfamiliar, not yet classified as hostile."
Ask for a record
"Please provide the relevant maintenance approval."
Keep time consistent
"Both timestamps are reported in UTC."
Reject premature closure
"The maintenance window alone does not close the alert."
Name the next check
"We will compare the ticket, account, and host."
Close the handoff
"S14 remains under investigation with a documented visibility gap."
6 language and precision notes
Successful
Describes the sign-in result, not permission or intent.
Unfamiliar
A comparison with known patterns, not proof of hostility.
Confirmed
Use only when the stated conclusion has supporting evidence.
No evidence found
Name the sources, period, and missing observations.
May be
Introduces a possibility without treating it as established.
Escalate
Means involve the required authority, not necessarily declare a breach.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff best fits S14?

Answer and explanation

D. Successful privileged sign-in; authorization unverified; application-log gap; escalated for investigation.

The correct summary preserves all four facts. The alternatives invent authorization, erase the visibility gap, or delay the required escalation.

2. Which record would help test the maintenance explanation?

Answer and explanation

A. A ticket matching this host, account, and authorized task

A relevant authorization record must connect to this event rather than merely exist.

3. Why is the log gap relevant?

Answer and explanation

D. It limits what the team can exclude about application activity.

Missing visibility limits negative conclusions without proving why the records are absent.

4. Which wording avoids premature blame?

Answer and explanation

A. The account was used; the operator is not yet verified.

Account activity does not by itself identify the person or their intent.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

A successful login is not verified authorization

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Anika: Security analyst / Luis: Shift lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • anomaly
  • correlation
  • confidence level
  • hypothesis
  • authentication
  • visibility gap
  • case record
  • event
  • triage
  • authorization
  1. Anika
    S14 triggered on a privileged sign-in at two fourteen UTC. The address is unfamiliar. I have the identity record, but not an explanation yet.
  2. Luis
    Start with the 1, then tell me what remains unknown. A successful sign-in matters, but I do not want the handoff to imply confirmed hostile activity.
  3. Anika
    There is a maintenance ticket from two to three UTC. The timing overlaps, although I have not matched its host or account to this record.
  4. Luis
    Then maintenance is a 2, not a closure reason. We need the actual task, operator, and asset match before describing this particular sign-in as approved work.
  5. Anika
    I nearly waited for the owner to reply before calling you. Should I escalate now and keep the maintenance explanation provisional?
  6. Luis
    Keep 3 moving. Our process requires escalation now, and your uncertainty belongs in the update. We can investigate without accusing the owner or announcing a confirmed intrusion.
  7. Anika
    I also found missing application logs between two ten and two twenty UTC. That interval includes the identity event we are discussing.
  8. Luis
    Record the 4 explicitly. We cannot use an incomplete application search to say that nothing happened after the sign-in, even if the records we have look ordinary.
  9. Anika
    The dashboard says new address. Is that enough to label the source hostile, or should I just report that it is unfamiliar?
  10. Luis
    Call it an 5. It deserves investigation, but novelty does not establish who controlled the connection. The maintenance explanation still needs testing, just as the hostile explanation does.
  11. Anika
    Understood. I will say that the identity log records success, the source is unfamiliar, and the account holder has not confirmed the activity.
  12. Luis
    Separate 6 from permission. The recorded login result does not establish that the operator was entitled to perform the particular task at that time.
  13. Anika
    I will request the relevant approval through our internal channel. The ticket number alone is not enough without checking which account and host it covers.
  14. Luis
    Exactly. We need 7 evidence tied to the action. Keep the original event details and identify any later corrections instead of silently rewriting the first report.
  15. Anika
    For the next shift, I will list the identity record, maintenance ticket, unanswered owner request, and missing application interval together in S14.
  16. Luis
    Put that in the 8. The next analyst should see both the current evidence and the reason this alert remains open, without reconstructing the conversation.
  17. Anika
    I will compare the ticket against the host and account, then update the working explanation when that evidence arrives. No malicious action is confirmed.
  18. Luis
    Describe your 9 carefully. We have a real observation and unresolved context. Repeating the same possibility does not turn it into stronger evidence.
  19. Anika
    My handoff will keep the event time in UTC and state that the missing application records limit our ability to exclude later activity.
  20. Luis
    Good. Continue the 10 and keep me involved under the escalation process. The status is under investigation, with authorization unverified and the visibility limitation clearly recorded.

Answers and explanations
  1. event Turn 2. The event is the recorded sign-in, distinct from the conclusion that it was malicious.
  2. hypothesis Turn 4. Maintenance is a possible explanation because the timing overlaps, but the relevant match is unverified.
  3. triage Turn 6. Triage is the initial assessment and handling process that continues while the owner response is pending.
  4. visibility gap Turn 8. The missing application logs create a visibility gap that restricts the conclusions available to investigators.
  5. anomaly Turn 10. An unfamiliar address departs from the expected pattern but does not establish malicious ownership or intent.
  6. authentication Turn 12. Authentication concerns the identity check reflected in the successful sign-in, not permission for every subsequent action.
  7. authorization Turn 14. Authorization is permission for the specific action, which requires more than a ticket with overlapping times.
  8. case record Turn 16. The case record preserves the evidence, unanswered questions, and actions for the next analyst.
  9. confidence level Turn 18. The confidence level qualifies the current interpretation and should change with evidence rather than with repeated assertion.
  10. correlation Turn 20. Correlation connects the identity event to relevant ticket and host records to test the competing explanations.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Anika: S14 triggered on a privileged sign-in at two fourteen UTC. The address is unfamiliar. I have the identity record, but not an explanation yet.
  2. Luis: Start with the event, then tell me what remains unknown. A successful sign-in matters, but I do not want the handoff to imply confirmed hostile activity.
  3. Anika: There is a maintenance ticket from two to three UTC. The timing overlaps, although I have not matched its host or account to this record.
  4. Luis: Then maintenance is a hypothesis, not a closure reason. We need the actual task, operator, and asset match before describing this particular sign-in as approved work.
  5. Anika: I nearly waited for the owner to reply before calling you. Should I escalate now and keep the maintenance explanation provisional?
  6. Luis: Keep triage moving. Our process requires escalation now, and your uncertainty belongs in the update. We can investigate without accusing the owner or announcing a confirmed intrusion.
  7. Anika: I also found missing application logs between two ten and two twenty UTC. That interval includes the identity event we are discussing.
  8. Luis: Record the visibility gap explicitly. We cannot use an incomplete application search to say that nothing happened after the sign-in, even if the records we have look ordinary.
  9. Anika: The dashboard says new address. Is that enough to label the source hostile, or should I just report that it is unfamiliar?
  10. Luis: Call it an anomaly. It deserves investigation, but novelty does not establish who controlled the connection. The maintenance explanation still needs testing, just as the hostile explanation does.
  11. Anika: Understood. I will say that the identity log records success, the source is unfamiliar, and the account holder has not confirmed the activity.
  12. Luis: Separate authentication from permission. The recorded login result does not establish that the operator was entitled to perform the particular task at that time.
  13. Anika: I will request the relevant approval through our internal channel. The ticket number alone is not enough without checking which account and host it covers.
  14. Luis: Exactly. We need authorization evidence tied to the action. Keep the original event details and identify any later corrections instead of silently rewriting the first report.
  15. Anika: For the next shift, I will list the identity record, maintenance ticket, unanswered owner request, and missing application interval together in S14.
  16. Luis: Put that in the case record. The next analyst should see both the current evidence and the reason this alert remains open, without reconstructing the conversation.
  17. Anika: I will compare the ticket against the host and account, then update the working explanation when that evidence arrives. No malicious action is confirmed.
  18. Luis: Describe your confidence level carefully. We have a real observation and unresolved context. Repeating the same possibility does not turn it into stronger evidence.
  19. Anika: My handoff will keep the event time in UTC and state that the missing application records limit our ability to exclude later activity.
  20. Luis: Good. Continue the correlation and keep me involved under the escalation process. The status is under investigation, with authorization unverified and the visibility limitation clearly recorded.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An alert is not confirmed account misuse

Fictional security triage using the lesson case: an alert concerns a privileged account, and authorization has not been established.

  1. Security analyst: The monitoring tool raised an alert involving a privileged account. Authorization is still unconfirmed.
  2. SOC lead: What did the security information and event management system, or SIEM, actually observe?
  3. Security analyst: It flagged account activity for review. I haven't checked the relevant authorization record yet.
  4. SOC lead: Then avoid describing the account as compromised simply because the detection rule triggered.
  5. Security analyst: Should I call it a false positive if the account owner says they recognize the activity?
  6. SOC lead: Treat that statement as evidence to verify, not automatic closure. Check it against the recorded event.
  7. Security analyst: I'll preserve the alert details and distinguish the observed activity from the owner's explanation.
  8. SOC lead: Good. Use the established triage process and keep any privileged-account details in the restricted case.
  9. Security analyst: I'll report authorization as unresolved until the review provides a supported conclusion.
  10. SOC lead: Then we can decide the next response step from evidence rather than from the alert label alone.
A travel explanation needs a matched timestamp

Fictional alert review: an account shows a login from an unfamiliar location; the employee mentioned travel, but dates have not been matched.

  1. Service desk analyst: The employee mentioned travel, so can we close the unfamiliar-location alert as a false positive?
  2. Security analyst: First match the travel explanation to the event time. We haven't established that connection yet.
  3. Service desk analyst: The location label itself might also be approximate rather than proof of physical presence.
  4. Security analyst: Correct. It's an observed system label, not a complete explanation of who used the account.
  5. Service desk analyst: What should I hand over without making the employee sound either guilty or automatically cleared?
  6. Security analyst: The event details, the employee's statement, and the unresolved timestamp comparison through the case channel.
  7. Service desk analyst: Should I ask them to send credentials so we can test their explanation directly?
  8. Security analyst: No. Use the approved verification process; credentials don't belong in this investigation conversation.
  9. Service desk analyst: I'll record the explanation as unverified context and keep the alert open for triage.
  10. Security analyst: Thank you. We'll assess authorization using the relevant evidence and avoid unsupported identity claims.
Many alerts may describe one event

Fictional monitoring review: three SIEM alerts reference the same event identifier; their relationship has not been assessed.

  1. Operations manager: Three alerts arrived together. Should I report three separate security incidents to leadership?
  2. Security analyst: Not yet. They reference the same event identifier, so we need to assess their relationship.
  3. Operations manager: Does the shared identifier prove all three are duplicates and can be dismissed?
  4. Security analyst: No. It suggests a connection, but each rule may describe a different aspect of the activity.
  5. Operations manager: Then alert count and incident count are different measures in the triage summary.
  6. Security analyst: Exactly. We have three alerts under investigation, not three independently confirmed incidents.
  7. Operations manager: Can you preserve their rule details while explaining that distinction in plain language?
  8. Security analyst: I'll retain the source records and say several detections may relate to one underlying event.
  9. Operations manager: Please avoid presenting either compromise or false-positive status before the relevant checks are complete.
  10. Security analyst: Agreed. I'll report the observed relationship and the open authorization question at the next checkpoint.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A successful login is not verified authorization
  1. Read turns 1-10. Stress observed facts separately from the maintenance hypothesis.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep authentication distinct from authorization.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Overlap is still not a match

Alert T8 records a sign-in at 05:20 UTC. Maintenance was scheduled from 05:00-06:00, but the account match is unverified. Application logs are unavailable. The shift lead has received the escalation.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The event time is 05:20 ___."

Answer and explanation

A. UTC

The briefing explicitly gives the event time in Coordinated Universal Time.

2. Lead: "The account match remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unverified

The overlapping maintenance period does not establish the relevant account match.

3. Analyst: "Application logs are ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unavailable

The supplied absence of application logs limits the investigation evidence.

4. Lead: "The escalation has been ___."

Answer and explanation

A. received

The briefing says the shift lead has already received the escalation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cybersecurity English
Professional audience: security analysts, SOC staff, incident responders, security engineers, GRC specialists, identity teams, vulnerability managers, and security leaders
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not a security certification, incident-response playbook, legal opinion, or authorization to test systems. Lesson 01: Security Triage and Alert Investigation
Original fictional case: Analyst Anika reviews alert S14 with shift lead Luis. The identity log records a successful privileged-account sign-in at 02:14 UTC from an unfamiliar address. A maintenance ticket covers 02:00-03:00 UTC, but its host and account have not been matched to the event. The account owner has not replied. Application logs are missing for 02:10-02:20 UTC. No malicious action is confirmed. The local process requires escalation to Luis now while correlation continues.
Language workshop: A successful login is not verified authorization
Communication goal: Give a concise alert handoff that separates logged events, plausible explanations, and unresolved authorization.
Language guidance: A successful authentication does not establish that the account holder intended or authorized the action. A matching time window supports a possible maintenance explanation, not a confirmed match to this event.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The identity log records a successful sign-in at 02:14 UTC." / "The event involves a privileged account." / "Authorization is not yet verified." / "The timing overlaps planned maintenance." / "We have not matched the host and account." / "Application logs are missing for ten minutes." / "Who can confirm the authorized operator?" / "I am escalating while correlation continues." / "No malicious action is confirmed at this point." / "That conclusion applies only to the sources checked." / "The address is unfamiliar, not yet classified as hostile." / "Please provide the relevant maintenance approval." / "Both timestamps are reported in UTC." / "The maintenance window alone does not close the alert." / "We will compare the ticket, account, and host." / "S14 remains under investigation with a documented visibility gap."
Vocabulary: alert: A notification that a detection condition was met. / event: An observable occurrence recorded by a system. / incident: An occurrence assessed as compromising or threatening security. / triage: Initial assessment to decide priority and next handling. / privileged account: An identity with elevated system permissions. / successful sign-in: A recorded completion of an authentication process. / authentication: Checking evidence that an identity claim is valid. / authorization: Permission for a particular action or resource. / source address: A network address associated with an observed connection. / timestamp: A recorded time associated with an event. / UTC: Coordinated Universal Time, a shared time reference. / maintenance window: A defined period for planned maintenance work. / correlation: Relating records to assess whether events are connected. / log source: A system or service producing event records. / telemetry: System observations collected for monitoring or analysis. / visibility gap: A period or area lacking needed observation data. / baseline: A reference pattern used to evaluate current behavior. / anomaly: A departure from an expected pattern. / false positive: A detection that incorrectly indicates the target condition. / benign activity: Activity assessed as not harmful in its context. / indicator: An observable sign that can support an investigation. / hypothesis: A proposed explanation awaiting evaluation. / confidence level: A qualified assessment of evidential strength. / case record: A documented account of observations and handling.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress observed facts separately from the maintenance hypothesis. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep authentication distinct from authorization. Check the transfer. Read the UTC time and the two missing-evidence statements clearly.
Partner: Anika: Security analyst / Luis: Shift lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Alert T8 records a sign-in at 05:20 UTC. Maintenance was scheduled from 05:00-06:00, but the account match is unverified. Application logs are unavailable. The shift lead has received the escalation.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Anika: I will compare the ticket against the host and account, then update the working explanation when that evidence arrives. No malicious action is confirmed.
Luis: Describe your confidence level carefully. We have a real observation and unresolved context. Repeating the same possibility does not turn it into stronger evidence.
Anika: My handoff will keep the event time in UTC and state that the missing application records limit our ability to exclude later activity.
Luis: Good. Continue the correlation and keep me involved under the escalation process. The status is under investigation, with authorization unverified and the visibility limitation clearly recorded.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Communication goal: Give a concise alert handoff that separates logged events, plausible explanations, and unresolved authorization.
Language guidance: A successful authentication does not establish that the account holder intended or authorized the action. A matching time window supports a possible maintenance explanation, not a confirmed match to this event.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The identity log records a successful sign-in at 02:14 UTC." / "The event involves a privileged account." / "Authorization is not yet verified." / "The timing overlaps planned maintenance." / "We have not matched the host and account." / "Application logs are missing for ten minutes." / "Who can confirm the authorized operator?" / "I am escalating while correlation continues." / "No malicious action is confirmed at this point." / "That conclusion applies only to the sources checked." / "The address is unfamiliar, not yet classified as hostile." / "Please provide the relevant maintenance approval." / "Both timestamps are reported in UTC." / "The maintenance window alone does not close the alert." / "We will compare the ticket, account, and host." / "S14 remains under investigation with a documented visibility gap."
Vocabulary: alert: A notification that a detection condition was met. / event: An observable occurrence recorded by a system. / incident: An occurrence assessed as compromising or threatening security. / triage: Initial assessment to decide priority and next handling. / privileged account: An identity with elevated system permissions. / successful sign-in: A recorded completion of an authentication process. / authentication: Checking evidence that an identity claim is valid. / authorization: Permission for a particular action or resource. / source address: A network address associated with an observed connection. / timestamp: A recorded time associated with an event. / UTC: Coordinated Universal Time, a shared time reference. / maintenance window: A defined period for planned maintenance work. / correlation: Relating records to assess whether events are connected. / log source: A system or service producing event records. / telemetry: System observations collected for monitoring or analysis. / visibility gap: A period or area lacking needed observation data. / baseline: A reference pattern used to evaluate current behavior. / anomaly: A departure from an expected pattern. / false positive: A detection that incorrectly indicates the target condition. / benign activity: Activity assessed as not harmful in its context. / indicator: An observable sign that can support an investigation. / hypothesis: A proposed explanation awaiting evaluation. / confidence level: A qualified assessment of evidential strength. / case record: A documented account of observations and handling.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress observed facts separately from the maintenance hypothesis. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep authentication distinct from authorization. Check the transfer. Read the UTC time and the two missing-evidence statements clearly.
Partner: Anika: Security analyst / Luis: Shift lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Alert T8 records a sign-in at 05:20 UTC. Maintenance was scheduled from 05:00-06:00, but the account match is unverified. Application logs are unavailable. The shift lead has received the escalation.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Anika: I will compare the ticket against the host and account, then update the working explanation when that evidence arrives. No malicious action is confirmed.
Luis: Describe your confidence level carefully. We have a real observation and unresolved context. Repeating the same possibility does not turn it into stronger evidence.
Anika: My handoff will keep the event time in UTC and state that the missing application records limit our ability to exclude later activity.
Luis: Good. Continue the correlation and keep me involved under the escalation process. The status is under investigation, with authorization unverified and the visibility limitation clearly recorded.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

02Incident Response and ContainmentIsolation is complete; the investigation is not+

Your goal

Coordinate a response update with action status, operational impact, evidence preservation, and a defined next report.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Isolation is complete; the investigation is not

Incident coordinator Mara briefs service owner Joel about suspected unauthorized activity on billing host B7, first reported at 09:10 UTC. Under the approved playbook, Mara authorized network isolation at 09:18; completion was verified at 09:22. Billing jobs are paused. Investigators are preserving available evidence; cause, data access, and wider scope remain unconfirmed. Restoration requires the response lead and service owner under the local process. The next update is due at 09:45, not a promised restoration time.

Mara: Incident coordinator / Joel: Billing service owner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When was isolation completion verified?

Answer and explanation

B. 09:22 UTC

The case distinguishes the first report, authorization, completion, and next update.

2. What is the confirmed operational impact?

Answer and explanation

D. Billing jobs are paused.

The supplied service effect is paused billing jobs, not wider damage.

3. What does 09:45 represent?

Answer and explanation

C. The next update time

The briefing expressly makes 09:45 a communication commitment, not a recovery promise.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

09:18 is the authorization time; 09:22 is verified completion. Saying isolated at 09:18 would merge two different events. Record both so later reviewers can reconstruct what actually happened.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
incident response
Coordinated handling of a suspected or confirmed security incident.coordinate incident response
containment
Action intended to limit incident spread or impact.verify containment actions
network isolation
Restricting a system's network connections.confirm network isolation
eradication
Removal of incident causes or persistence as applicable.verify eradication
recovery
Restoring affected capabilities under the required checks.authorize recovery
response lead
The person directing the assigned response process.brief the response lead
service owner
The role responsible for a business or technical service.involve the service owner
playbook
Agreed guidance for handling a defined situation.follow the approved playbook
action log
A time-ordered record of response actions.maintain the action log
evidence preservation
Protecting relevant records and artifacts from loss or alteration.coordinate evidence preservation
chain of custody
Documentation of evidence handling and transfers.maintain chain of custody
volatile data
Information that may be lost when system state changes.preserve volatile data appropriately
affected host
A system within the known impact or investigation scope.identify the affected host
incident scope
The systems, accounts, and effects covered by an incident assessment.establish incident scope
lateral movement
Movement from an initial foothold to other systems.investigate possible lateral movement
persistence
A means of retaining access across changes or restarts.assess persistence
exfiltration
Unauthorized transfer of data outside the relevant boundary.investigate possible exfiltration
restoration criterion
A condition required before returning a service.confirm restoration criteria
business impact
A consequence for organizational operations or objectives.report business impact
status update
A time-specific summary of the current position.issue a status update
decision authority
The role permitted to approve a particular action.confirm decision authority
handover
Transfer of responsibility and relevant information.complete the response handover
post-incident review
A review of events and improvements after handling an incident.schedule a post-incident review
root cause
An underlying reason supported by investigation evidence.establish the root cause

Isolation may limit exposure but does not establish the full incident scope, removal of persistence, or readiness to restore. Here, the next update is fixed while the recovery time remains unknown.

C Notice the language

Be decisive about the action, exact about the result

During a response, colleagues need short updates that they can repeat accurately. Separate the completed action from what it has not established. A calm statement of uncertainty is more useful than either dramatic speculation or premature reassurance.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the current impact
"Billing jobs are paused on B7."
Give the first report
"The activity was first reported at 09:10 UTC."
Separate approval from action
"Isolation was authorized at 09:18."
Confirm execution
"Completion was verified at 09:22."
Name the unknown
"The cause and wider scope remain unconfirmed."
Avoid a false all-clear
"Containment does not establish recovery readiness."
Protect the record
"Evidence preservation is in progress."
Assign the decision
"Restoration requires the response lead and service owner."
Bound the statement
"We have not confirmed unauthorized data access."
Commit to communication
"The next update will be at 09:45."
Correct the date meaning
"That is an update time, not a restoration estimate."
Acknowledge disruption
"I recognize the impact on billing operations."
Keep actions traceable
"Please record the authorization and completion separately."
Avoid an improvised change
"Use the response process before changing system state."
Prepare the handover
"Include completed actions, open questions, and owners."
Close with the current status
"B7 is isolated; investigation and recovery assessment continue."
6 language and precision notes
Reported at
Not necessarily the time the underlying activity began.
Authorized
Permission was given; execution still needs verification.
Contained
Define what action and scope support this description.
Compromised
Do not replace suspected activity with a stronger conclusion.
Restore
Requires the relevant technical and operational checks.
Next update
A commitment to communicate, not a promise of resolution.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which timeline is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Report 09:10; isolation authorized 09:18; completion verified 09:22.

The correct timeline preserves the distinct states and supplied timestamps.

2. Which reply addresses pressure to restore now?

Answer and explanation

C. Billing is paused; restoration still requires the specified response and service-owner process.

Operational urgency does not remove the restoration conditions stated in the case.

3. Which statement about data access is justified?

Answer and explanation

A. Unauthorized data access has not been confirmed.

The case leaves data access unresolved rather than establishing either extreme.

4. What belongs in the handover?

Answer and explanation

B. Actions, timestamps, impact, open questions, and responsible roles

These items preserve usable continuity without inventing conclusions or authority.

E Conversations

Isolation is complete; the investigation is not

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mara: Incident coordinator / Joel: Billing service owner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • status update
  • decision authority
  • Evidence preservation
  • Recovery
  • business impact
  • handover
  • Eradication
  • incident scope
  • action log
  • containment
  1. Joel
    Billing is paused, and the team wants a restoration time. We know B7 is isolated. Can I tell them the incident is now over?
  2. Mara
    No. The 1 action is complete, but cause, data access, and wider scope remain unconfirmed. We should be precise about what that completed action means.
  3. Joel
    Give me the timeline so I can explain the interruption. I have nine ten in the report and nine eighteen in the response channel.
  4. Mara
    The 2 should show first report at nine ten, isolation authorization at nine eighteen, and verified completion at nine twenty-two, all UTC.
  5. Joel
    I put nine eighteen as the isolation time in my update. That was only permission to act. I will change it to nine twenty-two.
  6. Mara
    Please also keep the 3 concrete: billing jobs are paused. We have not established that every related service is affected or that customer data left the environment.
  7. Joel
    Would restarting B7 help us resume sooner? Someone suggested it in the operations channel, but I do not know what investigators still need.
  8. Mara
    Route that request through the response team. 4 is in progress, and an improvised state change could affect the investigation. We must coordinate recovery and evidence needs.
  9. Joel
    I will stop treating restart as a routine operations decision here. Who needs to agree before this service can return under our process?
  10. Mara
    The 5 includes the response lead and you as service owner. Technical checks and operational consequences need to be assessed together before restoration.
  11. Joel
    I can explain the business impact and available operational options. I cannot certify from isolation alone that the underlying problem has been removed.
  12. Mara
    Correct. 6 has not been confirmed. We also cannot infer the absence of earlier unauthorized data access from the fact that connections are restricted now.
  13. Joel
    The team has also asked whether other hosts are involved. I only have the report about B7, so I have not named additional systems.
  14. Mara
    Keep the 7 open in the update. One known host does not prove that there are no others, but uncertainty is not evidence that all systems are affected.
  15. Joel
    What can I promise for nine forty-five: an update, or a working service? People will read the time as a restoration deadline unless I spell it out.
  16. Mara
    Yes, as a 8. We will report what changed, what remains unresolved, and the next actions then. That time is not a restoration estimate.
  17. Joel
    I will say that plainly. I also need the next shift to understand what was authorized and which checks are still open when they arrive.
  18. Mara
    Use the 9 record, including timestamps, owners, and outstanding decisions. They should not have to infer permission from a short chat message or an optimistic headline.
  19. Joel
    My message will say B7 is isolated, billing jobs remain paused, and investigation continues. I will reserve any all-clear for the authorized process.
  20. Mara
    That is accurate. 10 remains subject to the required checks and joint decision. We will give the next factual update at nine forty-five even if uncertainty remains.

Answers and explanations
  1. containment Turn 2. Containment aims to limit impact; it does not establish that investigation, eradication, or recovery is complete.
  2. action log Turn 4. The action log records the report, authorization, and verified execution as separate events.
  3. business impact Turn 6. The confirmed business impact is paused billing jobs, not an assumed broader outage or data loss.
  4. Evidence preservation Turn 8. Evidence preservation protects relevant information from loss or alteration while response decisions are coordinated.
  5. decision authority Turn 10. The local process assigns restoration decisions to the response lead and service owner, not any individual requesting speed.
  6. Eradication Turn 12. Eradication concerns removing the incident cause or persistence as applicable and is distinct from isolating a host.
  7. incident scope Turn 14. Incident scope covers the systems and effects under investigation; it remains incomplete in this case.
  8. status update Turn 16. The agreed status update commits the team to communicate at 09:45 without promising restoration by then.
  9. handover Turn 18. The handover transfers the current evidence, decisions, impact, and outstanding work to the incoming team.
  10. Recovery Turn 20. Recovery is the controlled restoration of affected capabilities and is not established merely by isolation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Joel: Billing is paused, and the team wants a restoration time. We know B7 is isolated. Can I tell them the incident is now over?
  2. Mara: No. The containment action is complete, but cause, data access, and wider scope remain unconfirmed. We should be precise about what that completed action means.

  6. Mara: Please also keep the business impact concrete: billing jobs are paused. We have not established that every related service is affected or that customer data left the environment.

  8. Mara: Route that request through the response team. Evidence preservation is in progress, and an improvised state change could affect the investigation. We must coordinate recovery and evidence needs.
  9. Joel: I will stop treating restart as a routine operations decision here. Who needs to agree before this service can return under our process?
  10. Mara: The decision authority includes the response lead and you as service owner. Technical checks and operational consequences need to be assessed together before restoration.
  11. Joel: I can explain the business impact and available operational options. I cannot certify from isolation alone that the underlying problem has been removed.
  12. Mara: Correct. Eradication has not been confirmed. We also cannot infer the absence of earlier unauthorized data access from the fact that connections are restricted now.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Handoff with a timestamp, not a confirmed cause

Separate fictional response handoff: a suspected incident was reported at 09:10 UTC on 8 September 2026 for service DOC-7. The cause and containment status are unconfirmed.

  1. Triage analyst: I'm handing over a suspected incident reported at 09:10 UTC on 8 September 2026 for service DOC-7.
  2. Incident coordinator: Please give me the source report and identify which parts are direct observations versus interpretation.
  3. Triage analyst: The source report identifies DOC-7 and that timestamp. The cause remains unconfirmed and needs investigation.
  4. Incident coordinator: Has containment been authorized or performed, or would that be another assumption in this handoff?
  5. Triage analyst: I haven't verified containment status. I'll mark it unknown rather than imply action occurred.
  6. Incident coordinator: Good. Include the reporting contact so we can clarify that through the established response process.
  7. Triage analyst: I'll keep evidence in the restricted case and avoid relabeling this as ransomware without supporting findings.
  8. Incident coordinator: Please preserve the original record for the forensics team and identify any missing context explicitly.
  9. Triage analyst: Can you confirm receipt of the report and the open cause and containment questions?
  10. Incident coordinator: Received: DOC-7, 09:10 UTC, 8 September 2026. Cause and containment remain unconfirmed; I'll coordinate authorized responders.
A containment request is not a completed action

Fictional incident handoff: a request to isolate a workstation was submitted to authorized responders, but no execution confirmation has arrived.

  1. Security analyst: The isolation request was submitted to the response team, but I haven't received execution confirmation.
  2. Incident lead: Then the handoff must say containment requested, not workstation isolated. Is that your current evidence?
  3. Security analyst: Yes. The request record is available, while the actual containment state remains unconfirmed.
  4. Incident lead: Please identify the receiving responder and the affected asset through the restricted incident record.
  5. Security analyst: I'll include both. Should I infer completion because the request was acknowledged a few minutes later?
  6. Incident lead: No. Acknowledgment confirms receipt, not necessarily action. Ask the authorized team for the actual status.
  7. Security analyst: Understood. I'll also retain the original event references so the forensics review has a traceable starting point.
  8. Incident lead: Good. Avoid making independent operational changes while the response owner coordinates the action.
  9. Security analyst: I'll hand over the evidence links, request history, and unresolved execution confirmation now.
  10. Incident lead: Received. I'll follow up with the responder and report confirmed containment status when it is available.
The handoff crosses a time-zone boundary

Fictional incident transfer: a report says 09:10 without a time zone; the receiving team works in another region, and the source log is available.

  1. Incident coordinator: The report lists nine ten, but it doesn't specify the time zone. Can you clarify?
  2. Monitoring analyst: I need to check the source log. I shouldn't assume it uses my local time.
  3. Incident coordinator: Please preserve the original timestamp and record any conversion explicitly for the receiving team.
  4. Monitoring analyst: I'll do that. Until the check finishes, the event time is incomplete rather than precisely established.
  5. Incident coordinator: Does the report identify the affected service clearly enough for us to locate the right case?
  6. Monitoring analyst: Yes, the service identifier is recorded. I'll include that and the source-log reference in the handoff.
  7. Incident coordinator: Please keep the suspected cause separate; matching timestamps won't establish attribution by itself.
  8. Monitoring analyst: Agreed. The cause remains unconfirmed, and no new containment status has been verified here.
  9. Incident coordinator: I'll acknowledge the case now, with timestamp interpretation and containment status listed as open questions.
  10. Monitoring analyst: Thank you. I'll send the verified time context through the same case channel once checked.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Isolation is complete; the investigation is not
  1. Read turns 1-10. Give report, authorization, and completion times as separate events.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress that the next update is not a recovery promise.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A report time is not a recovery deadline

Host C4 was first reported at 11:00 UTC. Isolation was authorized at 11:05 and verified complete at 11:09. The next update is 11:30. Recovery has not been authorized.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "Isolation was authorized at ___ UTC."

Answer and explanation

A. 11:05

The briefing assigns 11:05 to authorization rather than to completion.

2. Owner: "Completion was verified at ___ UTC."

Answer and explanation

C. 11:09

The verified completion occurred four minutes after the recorded authorization.

3. Coordinator: "Our next update is at ___ UTC."

Answer and explanation

D. 11:30

The supplied time is a communication commitment, not automatic recovery permission.

4. Owner: "Recovery remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unauthorized

The briefing explicitly states that recovery has not been authorized.

Lesson 02: Incident Response and Containment
Original fictional case: Incident coordinator Mara briefs service owner Joel about suspected unauthorized activity on billing host B7, first reported at 09:10 UTC. Under the approved playbook, Mara authorized network isolation at 09:18; completion was verified at 09:22. Billing jobs are paused. Investigators are preserving available evidence; cause, data access, and wider scope remain unconfirmed. Restoration requires the response lead and service owner under the local process. The next update is due at 09:45, not a promised restoration time.
Language workshop: Isolation is complete; the investigation is not
Communication goal: Coordinate a response update with action status, operational impact, evidence preservation, and a defined next report.
Language guidance: 09:18 is the authorization time; 09:22 is verified completion. Saying isolated at 09:18 would merge two different events. Record both so later reviewers can reconstruct what actually happened.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Billing jobs are paused on B7." / "The activity was first reported at 09:10 UTC." / "Isolation was authorized at 09:18." / "Completion was verified at 09:22." / "The cause and wider scope remain unconfirmed." / "Containment does not establish recovery readiness." / "Evidence preservation is in progress." / "Restoration requires the response lead and service owner." / "We have not confirmed unauthorized data access." / "The next update will be at 09:45." / "That is an update time, not a restoration estimate." / "I recognize the impact on billing operations." / "Please record the authorization and completion separately." / "Use the response process before changing system state." / "Include completed actions, open questions, and owners." / "B7 is isolated; investigation and recovery assessment continue."
Vocabulary: incident response: Coordinated handling of a suspected or confirmed security incident. / containment: Action intended to limit incident spread or impact. / network isolation: Restricting a system's network connections. / eradication: Removal of incident causes or persistence as applicable. / recovery: Restoring affected capabilities under the required checks. / response lead: The person directing the assigned response process. / service owner: The role responsible for a business or technical service. / playbook: Agreed guidance for handling a defined situation. / action log: A time-ordered record of response actions. / evidence preservation: Protecting relevant records and artifacts from loss or alteration. / chain of custody: Documentation of evidence handling and transfers. / volatile data: Information that may be lost when system state changes. / affected host: A system within the known impact or investigation scope. / incident scope: The systems, accounts, and effects covered by an incident assessment. / lateral movement: Movement from an initial foothold to other systems. / persistence: A means of retaining access across changes or restarts. / exfiltration: Unauthorized transfer of data outside the relevant boundary. / restoration criterion: A condition required before returning a service. / business impact: A consequence for organizational operations or objectives. / status update: A time-specific summary of the current position. / decision authority: The role permitted to approve a particular action. / handover: Transfer of responsibility and relevant information. / post-incident review: A review of events and improvements after handling an incident. / root cause: An underlying reason supported by investigation evidence.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Give report, authorization, and completion times as separate events. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress that the next update is not a recovery promise. Check the transfer. Read the three times without swapping authorization and execution.
Partner: Mara: Incident coordinator / Joel: Billing service owner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Host C4 was first reported at 11:00 UTC. Isolation was authorized at 11:05 and verified complete at 11:09. The next update is 11:30. Recovery has not been authorized.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Joel: I will say that plainly. I also need the next shift to understand what was authorized and which checks are still open when they arrive.
Mara: Use the handover record, including timestamps, owners, and outstanding decisions. They should not have to infer permission from a short chat message or an optimistic headline.
Joel: My message will say B7 is isolated, billing jobs remain paused, and investigation continues. I will reserve any all-clear for the authorized process.
Mara: That is accurate. Recovery remains subject to the required checks and joint decision. We will give the next factual update at nine forty-five even if uncertainty remains.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
