03Vulnerability ManagementThe same score, a different repair priority+

Your goal

Explain a risk-based remediation order without treating severity scores as complete business-risk assessments.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

The same score, a different repair priority

Vulnerability analyst Chen and infrastructure owner Petra review two fictional findings, each with a base severity score of 8.8. A affects an internet-facing production gateway; a validated threat bulletin reports exploitation of that flaw elsewhere. B affects an isolated test server containing synthetic data, with no route from the internet. No local exploitation is confirmed. Their agreed rule prioritizes demonstrated exploitation and external exposure when scores tie. A patch is available for each; testing, change approval, and verification remain required.

Chen: Vulnerability analyst / Petra: Infrastructure owner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which finding comes first under the stated tie-break rule?

Answer and explanation

A. A, the exposed production gateway flaw

The stated rule favors A because external exposure and demonstrated exploitation are supplied.

2. What does the threat bulletin establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The flaw has been exploited elsewhere.

Exploitation elsewhere is relevant evidence but does not prove a local incident.

3. What remains required after selecting A first?

Answer and explanation

B. Testing, change approval, and verification

Prioritization orders the work without completing the required change and verification steps.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A base severity score describes vulnerability characteristics, not the complete risk to a particular organization. Exposure, affected assets, observed exploitation, and local controls can justify different priorities for equal scores.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
vulnerability
A weakness that can be exploited to harm security.assess a vulnerability
CVE
Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures, an identification system.reference a CVE identifier
CVSS
Common Vulnerability Scoring System, a severity framework.interpret a CVSS score
base score
A severity score based on intrinsic vulnerability characteristics.compare base scores
exploit
A means of taking advantage of a vulnerability.assess exploit availability
exploitation in the wild
Observed real-world use of a vulnerability.confirm exploitation in the wild
KEV catalog
CISA's list of vulnerabilities known to have been exploited.check the KEV catalog
internet-facing
Reachable through an internet-exposed service or interface.inventory internet-facing assets
asset criticality
The importance of an asset to organizational objectives.assess asset criticality
attack surface
The exposed points through which a system can be targeted.reduce the attack surface
reachability
Whether a relevant path to an asset or service exists.verify reachability
affected version
A software version within a vulnerability's stated scope.confirm the affected version
patch
A software update intended to correct a defect.validate a patch
remediation
Work to resolve an identified weakness or problem.prioritize remediation
mitigation
A measure intended to reduce exposure or impact.verify a mitigation
compensating control
An alternative safeguard addressing a defined control need.evaluate a compensating control
remediation window
An agreed period for completing corrective work.agree a remediation window
change approval
Permission to carry out a defined system change.obtain change approval
regression test
A check that a change has not broken existing behavior.run regression tests
rescan
A follow-up scan to reassess a finding.perform a rescan
false finding
A reported weakness not supported after validation.investigate a false finding
exception expiry
The date or condition ending an approved exception.track exception expiry
risk acceptance
An authorized decision to retain a specified risk.document risk acceptance
closure evidence
Records showing that the required completion conditions were met.attach closure evidence

A patch being available is not evidence that it has been deployed successfully. Separate selection, testing, authorization, installation, and verification. A missing local incident does not erase the supplied reasons for prompt remediation.

C Notice the language

Explain the ranking, not just the number

Operations teams need a reason to interrupt planned work. Connect the weakness to the affected asset, exposure, and exploitation evidence. A defensible priority is more persuasive than repeating a severity label while ignoring operational change controls.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the tie
"Both findings have a base score of 8.8."
Add the asset context
"A affects the production gateway."
Name the exposure
"That service is internet-facing."
Qualify the threat evidence
"The bulletin confirms exploitation elsewhere."
Preserve local uncertainty
"We have not confirmed local exploitation."
Apply the agreed rule
"Our tie-break rule prioritizes A."
Keep B visible
"B remains in the remediation queue."
Distinguish availability
"The patch is available, not yet deployed."
Keep change controls
"Priority does not replace testing or approval."
Ask about service impact
"What interruption does the approved change require?"
Specify verification
"Attach evidence that the affected condition is resolved."
Avoid an unsupported closure
"An installation message alone does not close the finding."
Bound a mitigation
"State which exposure the temporary control reduces."
Name the exception owner
"Any delay needs the required owner and expiry."
Clarify the decision
"We are selecting the work order, not declaring an incident."
Close with a handoff
"Record A first, B still open, and the remaining change steps."
6 language and precision notes
Severe
A characteristic assessment, not a complete priority decision.
Exploited elsewhere
Supports urgency without establishing local compromise.
Isolated
Verify the actual network and access boundaries.
Available
Does not mean tested, approved, or installed.
Resolved
Needs evidence against the finding's closure criteria.
Accept
A risk decision requiring the designated authority.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is strongest for A first?

Answer and explanation

A. Equal scores; A adds external exposure and validated exploitation evidence under the agreed rule.

The correct explanation uses the actual differentiators without inventing a score or incident.

2. Which statement about B is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. It is lower in this ordering but still requires handling.

The tie-break changes priority, not the existence or eventual handling of B.

3. What distinguishes a patch from a mitigation?

Answer and explanation

A. A patch changes software; another control may reduce exposure without correcting that software.

The distinction concerns how the weakness or its exposure is addressed.

4. Which closure message is supported before any deployment?

Answer and explanation

B. A is prioritized; deployment and verification are still pending.

Available patches and selected priorities do not establish completed remediation.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

The same score, a different repair priority

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Chen: Vulnerability analyst / Petra: Infrastructure owner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • regression test
  • internet-facing
  • risk acceptance
  • exploitation in the wild
  • change approval
  • base score
  • closure evidence
  • remediation
  • asset criticality
  • mitigation
  1. Petra
    Both findings are eight point eight. The operations queue usually sorts by score, so I need a clear reason to move the gateway ahead.
  2. Chen
    The 1 is the same, but our tie-break rule uses exposure and demonstrated exploitation. A has both of those additional facts in the supplied assessment.
  3. Petra
    Let us separate those facts. A is the production gateway reachable externally. B is an isolated test server holding synthetic rather than live customer data.
  4. Chen
    Yes. A is 2, while B has no route from the internet in this case. That changes the immediate exposure even before we discuss the bulletin.
  5. Petra
    The bulletin says the gateway flaw has been used against other organizations. Does that mean we should announce an incident in our own environment?
  6. Chen
    No. It establishes 3, not local compromise. We should preserve that distinction while using the verified information as a reason to prioritize corrective work.
  7. Petra
    Fine, A first. Can we leave B assigned and visible? I do not want lower priority to turn into nobody owning the test-server fix.
  8. Chen
    Keep it in the 4 queue. The ordering is A first under our agreed rule, not A matters and B never matters. Both need an accountable path.
  9. Petra
    A patch is available for each. The gateway update may interrupt production, so we still need the service team involved in timing and checks.
  10. Chen
    Obtain the required 5. Prioritizing A does not prove the update is compatible or authorize an unplanned production interruption. We need the relevant testing and operational coordination.
  11. Petra
    Then my ticket will say selected first, patch available, testing and approval pending. I will not mark it resolved just because we found an update.
  12. Chen
    Good. The 6 should cover the relevant existing service behavior. A security correction still needs evidence that the planned change works in the required environment.
  13. Petra
    If we cannot deploy immediately, the service owner may propose a temporary control. I want the wording to make clear what that would actually achieve.
  14. Chen
    Describe it as a 7 with a defined scope and verification. Do not claim that reducing one access path automatically corrects the underlying software or removes every route.
  15. Petra
    Any delay would need the owner, rationale, and review conditions required by our process. An informal note saying accepted would be too vague.
  16. Chen
    Exactly. Formal 8 is not something the analyst can invent to clear the queue. It must follow the assigned authority and state what remains exposed.
  17. Petra
    After installation, what evidence do you need to close the finding? I can attach the change record, but we still need to verify the affected condition.
  18. Chen
    Attach the 9 to the record. The person reviewing completion should see why the affected condition is considered resolved, not just that someone ran an installer.
  19. Petra
    My summary is A first for demonstrated exploitation and external exposure, B still open, and no local exploitation confirmed. The change steps remain required.
  20. Chen
    That makes the 10 and exposure visible alongside severity. We can justify urgency without exaggerating the evidence or treating the score as the whole decision.

Answers and explanations
  1. base score Turn 2. The base score reflects intrinsic severity characteristics and does not include every relevant local risk factor.
  2. internet-facing Turn 4. Internet-facing identifies A's external exposure, which differentiates it from the isolated test system.
  3. exploitation in the wild Turn 6. Real-world exploitation elsewhere supports prioritization but does not by itself establish local compromise.
  4. remediation Turn 8. Remediation is the corrective work still required for both findings, even though A comes first.
  5. change approval Turn 10. Change approval authorizes the defined operational change; a security priority does not automatically supply that permission.
  6. regression test Turn 12. A regression test checks that applying the change has not broken existing behavior required by the service.
  7. mitigation Turn 14. A mitigation reduces a defined exposure or impact and need not remove the underlying software weakness.
  8. risk acceptance Turn 16. Risk acceptance is an authorized decision concerning a specified retained risk, not an informal label that erases the weakness.
  9. closure evidence Turn 18. Closure evidence demonstrates that the required completion conditions were actually met after the change.
  10. asset criticality Turn 20. Asset criticality adds the importance of the production gateway to the context of the prioritization discussion.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Petra: Both findings are eight point eight. The operations queue usually sorts by score, so I need a clear reason to move the gateway ahead.
  2. Chen: The base score is the same, but our tie-break rule uses exposure and demonstrated exploitation. A has both of those additional facts in the supplied assessment.
  3. Petra: Let us separate those facts. A is the production gateway reachable externally. B is an isolated test server holding synthetic rather than live customer data.
  4. Chen: Yes. A is internet-facing, while B has no route from the internet in this case. That changes the immediate exposure even before we discuss the bulletin.
  5. Petra: The bulletin says the gateway flaw has been used against other organizations. Does that mean we should announce an incident in our own environment?
  6. Chen: No. It establishes exploitation in the wild, not local compromise. We should preserve that distinction while using the verified information as a reason to prioritize corrective work.
  7. Petra: Fine, A first. Can we leave B assigned and visible? I do not want lower priority to turn into nobody owning the test-server fix.
  8. Chen: Keep it in the remediation queue. The ordering is A first under our agreed rule, not A matters and B never matters. Both need an accountable path.
  9. Petra: A patch is available for each. The gateway update may interrupt production, so we still need the service team involved in timing and checks.
  10. Chen: Obtain the required change approval. Prioritizing A does not prove the update is compatible or authorize an unplanned production interruption. We need the relevant testing and operational coordination.
  11. Petra: Then my ticket will say selected first, patch available, testing and approval pending. I will not mark it resolved just because we found an update.
  12. Chen: Good. The regression test should cover the relevant existing service behavior. A security correction still needs evidence that the planned change works in the required environment.
  13. Petra: If we cannot deploy immediately, the service owner may propose a temporary control. I want the wording to make clear what that would actually achieve.
  14. Chen: Describe it as a mitigation with a defined scope and verification. Do not claim that reducing one access path automatically corrects the underlying software or removes every route.
  15. Petra: Any delay would need the owner, rationale, and review conditions required by our process. An informal note saying accepted would be too vague.
  16. Chen: Exactly. Formal risk acceptance is not something the analyst can invent to clear the queue. It must follow the assigned authority and state what remains exposed.
  17. Petra: After installation, what evidence do you need to close the finding? I can attach the change record, but we still need to verify the affected condition.
  18. Chen: Attach the closure evidence to the record. The person reviewing completion should see why the affected condition is considered resolved, not just that someone ran an installer.
  19. Petra: My summary is A first for demonstrated exploitation and external exposure, B still open, and no local exploitation confirmed. The change steps remain required.
  20. Chen: That makes the asset criticality and exposure visible alongside severity. We can justify urgency without exaggerating the evidence or treating the score as the whole decision.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Similar severity, different exposure

Fictional vulnerability prioritization using the lesson case: similar severity scores affect an exposed service and an isolated test system.

  1. Infrastructure manager: The two vulnerabilities have similar scores. Should they receive identical remediation priority?
  2. Vulnerability analyst: Not automatically. One affects an exposed service, while the other is on an isolated test system.
  3. Infrastructure manager: So the Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, score isn't our complete local risk assessment.
  4. Vulnerability analyst: Correct. Compare exposure, exploitability evidence, asset importance, and verified compensating controls for each case.
  5. Infrastructure manager: Can we assume the isolated system is harmless and postpone it indefinitely?
  6. Vulnerability analyst: No. Isolation is relevant context, not proof of zero risk or a substitute for reviewing its actual state.
  7. Infrastructure manager: Please avoid inventing active exploitation simply because the exposed service sounds more concerning.
  8. Vulnerability analyst: Agreed. I'll separate known exposure from unconfirmed exploitation and check the applicable vulnerability records.
  9. Infrastructure manager: Bring a ranked recommendation with those common criteria and any required remediation approvals.
  10. Vulnerability analyst: I will. The comparison will explain the tradeoff without reducing priority to one score.
A scanner finding needs applicability checks

Fictional assessment: a scanner flags the same vulnerability identifier on two servers; installed component and version evidence is incomplete for one.

  1. Server owner: Both servers show the same Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures identifier, or CVE. Are they equally affected?
  2. Vulnerability analyst: We need applicability evidence. One server's installed component and version record is still incomplete.
  3. Server owner: Would dismissing that finding until inventory is perfect be the simplest prioritization decision?
  4. Vulnerability analyst: No. Keep the unverified finding visible and request the missing inventory information through the normal process.
  5. Server owner: For the confirmed server, should we compare exposure and service importance while that check proceeds?
  6. Vulnerability analyst: Yes. Use the same criteria, but label unknown fields rather than filling them with reassuring assumptions.
  7. Server owner: We also have a claimed compensating control whose current configuration hasn't been verified.
  8. Vulnerability analyst: Then treat that control as unconfirmed, not as a demonstrated reduction in exploitability.
  9. Server owner: I'll supply the inventory record and route the control-verification request to its owner.
  10. Vulnerability analyst: I'll update the comparison when evidence arrives and keep scanner detection separate from confirmed applicability.
Patch availability and service disruption

Fictional remediation planning: two affected services have proposed patches; one has a tested maintenance plan, while the other's recovery plan is unverified.

  1. Operations lead: Both patches are available. Can we schedule both services together and close the vulnerability tickets?
  2. Security engineer: Patch availability isn't completed remediation. One service has a tested maintenance plan; the other's recovery plan is unverified.
  3. Operations lead: Should the unverified plan automatically put that service last, regardless of its exposure?
  4. Security engineer: No. We need to compare exposure and exploitability alongside change risk and recovery readiness.
  5. Operations lead: That creates a tradeoff between addressing security risk quickly and avoiding an unreviewed service change.
  6. Security engineer: Exactly. The authorized change and risk owners should assess the options using verified evidence.
  7. Operations lead: Can a proposed compensating control be listed while recovery planning is completed?
  8. Security engineer: As a proposal, yes. Don't present it as operating protection until its implementation and effect are checked.
  9. Operations lead: I'll bring the readiness gap and maintenance options to the responsible review rather than promise closure.
  10. Security engineer: I'll provide the vulnerability context and distinguish planned patching from verified remediation in the status.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The same score, a different repair priority
  1. Read turns 1-10. Stress equal scores, different exposure, and exploitation elsewhere.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep available, deployed, and verified distinct.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A high priority is not completed work

Findings X and Y have equal base scores. X is internet-facing with verified exploitation elsewhere; Y is isolated. The local rule selects X first. Its patch is available, but deployment has not started.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The first priority is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. X

The supplied tie-break rule favors the exposed flaw with demonstrated exploitation.

2. Owner: "The base scores are ___."

Answer and explanation

B. equal

The case explicitly makes the base severity scores the same.

3. Analyst: "Exploitation is verified ___."

Answer and explanation

C. elsewhere

The supplied evidence describes exploitation elsewhere, not a confirmed local incident.

4. Owner: "Deployment is still ___."

Answer and explanation

C. pending

The briefing says deployment has not started despite patch availability.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

All organizations, incidents, figures, priorities, and control criteria in the cases are invented.
Lesson 03: Vulnerability Management
Original fictional case: Vulnerability analyst Chen and infrastructure owner Petra review two fictional findings, each with a base severity score of 8.8. A affects an internet-facing production gateway; a validated threat bulletin reports exploitation of that flaw elsewhere. B affects an isolated test server containing synthetic data, with no route from the internet. No local exploitation is confirmed. Their agreed rule prioritizes demonstrated exploitation and external exposure when scores tie. A patch is available for each; testing, change approval, and verification remain required.
Language workshop: The same score, a different repair priority
Communication goal: Explain a risk-based remediation order without treating severity scores as complete business-risk assessments.
Language guidance: A base severity score describes vulnerability characteristics, not the complete risk to a particular organization. Exposure, affected assets, observed exploitation, and local controls can justify different priorities for equal scores.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Both findings have a base score of 8.8." / "A affects the production gateway." / "That service is internet-facing." / "The bulletin confirms exploitation elsewhere." / "We have not confirmed local exploitation." / "Our tie-break rule prioritizes A." / "B remains in the remediation queue." / "The patch is available, not yet deployed." / "Priority does not replace testing or approval." / "What interruption does the approved change require?" / "Attach evidence that the affected condition is resolved." / "An installation message alone does not close the finding." / "State which exposure the temporary control reduces." / "Any delay needs the required owner and expiry." / "We are selecting the work order, not declaring an incident." / "Record A first, B still open, and the remaining change steps."
Vocabulary: vulnerability: A weakness that can be exploited to harm security. / CVE: Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures, an identification system. / CVSS: Common Vulnerability Scoring System, a severity framework. / base score: A severity score based on intrinsic vulnerability characteristics. / exploit: A means of taking advantage of a vulnerability. / exploitation in the wild: Observed real-world use of a vulnerability. / KEV catalog: CISA's list of vulnerabilities known to have been exploited. / internet-facing: Reachable through an internet-exposed service or interface. / asset criticality: The importance of an asset to organizational objectives. / attack surface: The exposed points through which a system can be targeted. / reachability: Whether a relevant path to an asset or service exists. / affected version: A software version within a vulnerability's stated scope. / patch: A software update intended to correct a defect. / remediation: Work to resolve an identified weakness or problem. / mitigation: A measure intended to reduce exposure or impact. / compensating control: An alternative safeguard addressing a defined control need. / remediation window: An agreed period for completing corrective work. / change approval: Permission to carry out a defined system change. / regression test: A check that a change has not broken existing behavior. / rescan: A follow-up scan to reassess a finding. / false finding: A reported weakness not supported after validation. / exception expiry: The date or condition ending an approved exception. / risk acceptance: An authorized decision to retain a specified risk. / closure evidence: Records showing that the required completion conditions were met.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress equal scores, different exposure, and exploitation elsewhere. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep available, deployed, and verified distinct. Check the transfer. Read the priority without claiming a confirmed local compromise.
Partner: Chen: Vulnerability analyst / Petra: Infrastructure owner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Findings X and Y have equal base scores. X is internet-facing with verified exploitation elsewhere; Y is isolated. The local rule selects X first. Its patch is available, but deployment has not started.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Petra: After installation, what evidence do you need to close the finding? I can attach the change record, but we still need to verify the affected condition.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Identity, Access, and Least PrivilegeSupport needs logs, not a shared administrator+

Your goal

Negotiate a specific access arrangement using identity, task, permission, duration, approval, and accountability.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Support needs logs, not a shared administrator

Vendor coordinator Eva requests a shared administrator account so two technicians can diagnose a service issue. Access reviewer Rashid confirms that the supplied task needs only read access to sanitized diagnostic logs for two hours. Their local policy requires named identities, multifactor authentication, owner approval, and recorded expiry. No approval has been granted. The proposed alternative gives each technician only the required log access. Any later write task needs a separate assessed request; no broader privilege is justified by the present facts.

Eva: Vendor coordinator / Rashid: Access reviewer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the diagnostic task require?

Answer and explanation

A. Read access to sanitized logs for two hours

The briefing supplies a narrow read-only task with a defined two-hour duration.

2. Which local requirement is explicit?

Answer and explanation

A. Named identities with multifactor authentication, approval, and expiry

These four conditions are stated in the fictional local access policy.

3. What is the current approval status?

Answer and explanation

A. Not granted

The briefing explicitly says no approval has yet been granted.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Authentication checks identity; authorization controls permitted actions. Multifactor authentication does not by itself justify administrator rights. Sanitization also does not remove the stated approval, identity, and expiry requirements.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
identity and access management
Processes for managing identities and their permissions.review identity and access management
least privilege
Limiting access to what the authorized task requires.apply least privilege
named account
An account associated with an identified individual.provision a named account
shared account
An account used by more than one individual.review shared-account risk
privileged access
Permission to perform elevated system actions.control privileged access
read-only access
Permission to view data without changing it.grant read-only access
entitlement
A specific right or permission assigned to an identity.review entitlements
role-based access
Permissions assigned through defined job or task roles.configure role-based access
multifactor authentication
Verification using more than one distinct factor type.require multifactor authentication
single sign-on
Access to multiple services through a shared sign-in arrangement.support single sign-on
federation
Trust between identity systems to support identity assertions.configure identity federation
provisioning
Creating or enabling an identity or its access.complete access provisioning
deprovisioning
Removing or disabling access that is no longer needed.verify deprovisioning
access review
Evaluation of whether granted rights remain appropriate.conduct an access review
time-bound access
Permission with a defined end time or duration.approve time-bound access
expiry
The point at which a permission or approval ends.record access expiry
just-in-time access
Access activated only for an approved period of need.request just-in-time access
standing privilege
Elevated access retained beyond a particular active task.reduce standing privilege
session record
Information documenting a particular access session.review session records
accountability
Ability to associate actions with responsible identities.preserve individual accountability
separation of duties
Dividing sensitive responsibilities to reduce conflicting control.maintain separation of duties
break-glass access
Controlled emergency access outside the normal route.govern break-glass access
service account
An identity used by a service or automated process.manage service-account permissions
sanitized logs
Diagnostic records with restricted details removed or protected.share sanitized logs

A two-hour read-only task supports a bounded proposal, not automatic approval. If the work later changes, reassess the additional permission instead of silently extending the original scope.

C Notice the language

Offer a workable boundary

A useful access challenge connects the restriction to the task and offers a precise alternative. Avoid sounding as though vendor status itself makes someone untrustworthy. Explain which requested permissions are unnecessary and how the legitimate work can proceed through the approved route.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the support need
"We need to enable the diagnostic work."
Clarify the task
"Which action requires administrator rights?"
Use the supplied scope
"The task only needs sanitized diagnostic logs."
Offer narrower access
"Read-only access supports that task."
Preserve identity
"Each technician needs a named account."
State the local condition
"Multifactor authentication is required."
Bound the duration
"The requested access lasts two hours."
Keep approval explicit
"The service owner has not approved it yet."
Protect accountability
"We need to associate actions with each technician."
Avoid standing access
"This request does not justify permanent privilege."
Name expiry
"Record when the access ends."
Separate future work
"A later write task needs a new assessed request."
Avoid a personal judgment
"The restriction follows the task, not a judgment about the vendor."
Ask for evidence
"Please include the task, identities, and duration."
Confirm removal
"Verify that access ends as approved."
Close the proposal
"Submit named, read-only, time-bound access for owner review."
6 language and precision notes
Need
Link the permission to an actual task.
Shared
Can weaken individual attribution when people use one identity.
Read-only
Still requires appropriate handling and approval.
Temporary
Needs an explicit end, not an informal expectation.
Approved
Distinguish a proposal from a recorded decision.
More access
Requires a reason beyond convenience or a possible future task.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which proposal best matches the supplied task and policy?

Answer and explanation

A. Named read-only log access for two hours, with MFA and owner approval

The correct proposal preserves individual identity, task-limited permission, authentication, approval, and duration. Each alternative changes one required boundary.

2. Why does MFA not settle the request?

Answer and explanation

A. It strengthens identity verification but does not justify unnecessary permissions.

Authentication strength and permission scope address different questions in access decisions.

3. What should happen if a write task emerges?

Answer and explanation

A. Submit the additional need for a separate assessment.

The original facts justify only reading, and the briefing requires a separate request for writes.

4. Which explanation avoids blaming the vendor?

Answer and explanation

A. The task requires log reading, so we are proposing only that access.

The task-based explanation states a legitimate boundary without making a personal accusation.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

Support needs logs, not a shared administrator

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Eva: Vendor coordinator / Rashid: Access reviewer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • multifactor authentication
  • time-bound access
  • Provisioning
  • least privilege
  • entitlement
  • expiry
  • read-only access
  • named account
  • deprovisioning
  • Accountability
  1. Eva
    Two vendor technicians need to investigate the service issue. They asked for a shared administrator account so either person can begin when available.
  2. Rashid
    Let us start with 1. The supplied task needs read access to sanitized diagnostic logs for two hours. Which part requires changing the system or administering it?
  3. Eva
    None of the documented diagnostic steps requires a write. They want to inspect the logs and compare the recorded errors with their product knowledge.
  4. Rashid
    Then propose 2. That supports the described work without granting unrelated administrative rights. We should make the permitted action clear in the request.
  5. Eva
    They will take turns during the two hours. Can one account cover both, or do we need two identities even for that short window?
  6. Rashid
    Our policy requires a 3 for each technician. A private agreement about sharing does not replace individual attribution or the access conditions we have been asked to enforce.
  7. Eva
    I will collect both identities. The vendor also says their ordinary login is secure. Does that satisfy our sign-in requirement for this request?
  8. Rashid
    We require 4. We need to verify that the approved arrangement meets it, not rely on the general adjective secure or treat vendor familiarity as an exemption.
  9. Eva
    That gives me the identity and sign-in requirements. For timing, I can specify a two-hour window rather than leaving the access available indefinitely.
  10. Rashid
    Make it 5 with a recorded end. Short work does not justify standing privilege, and the duration should be visible to whoever reviews and implements the request.
  11. Eva
    I have submitted the request, but the owner has not signed off. What can I tell the technicians while that decision is pending?
  12. Rashid
    Correct. 6 follows the required approval. A well-scoped request is ready for review; it does not authorize itself simply because the proposed permissions are narrow.
  13. Eva
    If their diagnosis later shows that a configuration change is needed, I will ask them to describe the new action and why it is necessary.
  14. Rashid
    That is a new 7 question. Do not silently add write access to a request justified only by log reading. The additional need must go through its own assessment.
  15. Eva
    I can explain that this is about fitting access to work, not suggesting that the technicians intend to misuse anything. We still want their support.
  16. Rashid
    Exactly. 8 also protects legitimate work by showing who did what. Neutral language makes that purpose clearer than a blanket statement that vendors are risky.
  17. Eva
    I will put the identities, read-only log scope, multifactor requirement, two-hour duration, and owner decision in the request. What should I say about the end?
  18. Rashid
    Record the 9 and confirm how it will be enforced. The access should not remain active simply because everyone assumes somebody else will remove it later.
  19. Eva
    Then I will send the bounded proposal for owner review and avoid promising administrator access. Any changed task will return with a specific justification.
  20. Rashid
    Good. Verify 10 when the approved window ends. We can enable the diagnostic work while keeping permission, duration, approval, and individual responsibility explicit.

Answers and explanations
  1. least privilege Turn 2. Least privilege ties the permissions to the actual authorized task rather than to the broadest convenient account.
  2. read-only access Turn 4. Read-only access matches inspection of the supplied diagnostic logs without adding unsupported write or administrative rights.
  3. named account Turn 6. A named account associates access with an identified technician, as required by the stated local policy.
  4. multifactor authentication Turn 8. Multifactor authentication is the explicit local sign-in condition and is separate from the task's permission scope.
  5. time-bound access Turn 10. Time-bound access records a limited period of need instead of granting ongoing privilege for a short task.
  6. Provisioning Turn 12. Provisioning enables the access, but the case still requires owner approval before that approved arrangement can be implemented.
  7. entitlement Turn 14. An entitlement is a particular permission; adding write authority changes the access requested and needs reassessment.
  8. Accountability Turn 16. Accountability preserves a connection between actions and individual identities rather than making an accusation about intentions.
  9. expiry Turn 18. Expiry specifies when the permission ends, making the temporary access boundary concrete and reviewable.
  10. deprovisioning Turn 20. Deprovisioning removes or disables access after the approved need ends rather than leaving an unused permission active.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Eva: Two vendor technicians need to investigate the service issue. They asked for a shared administrator account so either person can begin when available.
  2. Rashid: Let us start with least privilege. The supplied task needs read access to sanitized diagnostic logs for two hours. Which part requires changing the system or administering it?
  3. Eva: None of the documented diagnostic steps requires a write. They want to inspect the logs and compare the recorded errors with their product knowledge.
  4. Rashid: Then propose read-only access. That supports the described work without granting unrelated administrative rights. We should make the permitted action clear in the request.
  5. Eva: They will take turns during the two hours. Can one account cover both, or do we need two identities even for that short window?
  6. Rashid: Our policy requires a named account for each technician. A private agreement about sharing does not replace individual attribution or the access conditions we have been asked to enforce.
  7. Eva: I will collect both identities. The vendor also says their ordinary login is secure. Does that satisfy our sign-in requirement for this request?
  8. Rashid: We require multifactor authentication. We need to verify that the approved arrangement meets it, not rely on the general adjective secure or treat vendor familiarity as an exemption.
  9. Eva: That gives me the identity and sign-in requirements. For timing, I can specify a two-hour window rather than leaving the access available indefinitely.
  10. Rashid: Make it time-bound access with a recorded end. Short work does not justify standing privilege, and the duration should be visible to whoever reviews and implements the request.
  11. Eva: I have submitted the request, but the owner has not signed off. What can I tell the technicians while that decision is pending?
  12. Rashid: Correct. Provisioning follows the required approval. A well-scoped request is ready for review; it does not authorize itself simply because the proposed permissions are narrow.
  13. Eva: If their diagnosis later shows that a configuration change is needed, I will ask them to describe the new action and why it is necessary.
  14. Rashid: That is a new entitlement question. Do not silently add write access to a request justified only by log reading. The additional need must go through its own assessment.
  15. Eva: I can explain that this is about fitting access to work, not suggesting that the technicians intend to misuse anything. We still want their support.
  16. Rashid: Exactly. Accountability also protects legitimate work by showing who did what. Neutral language makes that purpose clearer than a blanket statement that vendors are risky.
  17. Eva: I will put the identities, read-only log scope, multifactor requirement, two-hour duration, and owner decision in the request. What should I say about the end?
  18. Rashid: Record the expiry and confirm how it will be enforced. The access should not remain active simply because everyone assumes somebody else will remove it later.
  19. Eva: Then I will send the bounded proposal for owner review and avoid promising administrator access. Any changed task will return with a specific justification.
  20. Rashid: Good. Verify deprovisioning when the approved window ends. We can enable the diagnostic work while keeping permission, duration, approval, and individual responsibility explicit.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Request named, reviewed vendor access

Fictional access discussion using the lesson case: a vendor requests a shared privileged account, and the organization has a controlled access-review process.

  1. Vendor coordinator: Our support team wants one shared privileged account so anyone on the shift can help.
  2. IAM analyst: Please submit the named users, required tasks, and requested access period through our access-review process.
  3. Vendor coordinator: Why do you need the tasks instead of simply granting the administrator role we requested?
  4. IAM analyst: Identity and access management, or IAM, needs that context to assess least privilege for the actual work.
  5. Vendor coordinator: Would enabling multifactor authentication, or MFA, settle the shared-account concern by itself?
  6. IAM analyst: It wouldn't answer every question about individual accountability or appropriate permissions. Those still need review.
  7. Vendor coordinator: I'll identify the support tasks and users. Can you guarantee approval before tomorrow's shift?
  8. IAM analyst: I can request a review update, not promise approval or reviewer availability we haven't confirmed.
  9. Vendor coordinator: Please let us know which details are missing so we can respond through the same channel.
  10. IAM analyst: Agreed. We'll keep the request specific and avoid treating submission as approved privileged access.
A temporary role without an end date

Fictional access request: an employee needs temporary reporting access for a project, but the request does not specify tasks or duration.

  1. Project lead: Please give our analyst the temporary reporting role. We need the access for a project.
  2. Access administrator: Could you specify the reporting tasks and requested end date so the owner can review the scope?
  3. Project lead: The project schedule is still being agreed. Should I just request permanent access for convenience?
  4. Access administrator: Let's preserve that uncertainty and ask for a reviewed duration rather than silently change temporary into permanent.
  5. Project lead: Do you also need to know whether the analyst must view reports or change their configuration?
  6. Access administrator: Yes. Role-based access control, or RBAC, assigns permissions through roles, and those tasks may need different scopes.
  7. Project lead: I'll clarify the actual tasks and ask the project owner about the access period today.
  8. Access administrator: Please include the business purpose and required review timing without assuming the requested role will be approved.
  9. Project lead: Understood. We can track the request as pending while the owner checks the least-privilege fit.
  10. Access administrator: I'll confirm receipt and return any missing-information questions through the controlled access channel.
Check task needs before copying access

Fictional access review: a manager proposes copying a departing colleague's role for a replacement; task equivalence has not been checked.

  1. Team manager: Can you copy the departing colleague's access to the replacement so onboarding is faster?
  2. IAM reviewer: Please identify the replacement's actual tasks first. We haven't established that both roles need identical permissions.
  3. Team manager: They have the same job title. Isn't that enough to select the existing access profile?
  4. IAM reviewer: A title is useful context, but it doesn't verify every permission or any historical access exceptions.
  5. Team manager: Then I'll ask the system owners to confirm which functions are needed for the new assignment.
  6. IAM reviewer: Thank you. Include the requested start timing so the review can be coordinated realistically.
  7. Team manager: Would an existing MFA setup mean the permission review can be skipped for this account?
  8. IAM reviewer: No. Authentication and authorization answer different questions: who signs in and what they may do.
  9. Team manager: I'll submit the task-based request and keep any additional permissions pending owner review.
  10. IAM reviewer: I'll check the role mapping against that scope rather than reproduce unverified access by inheritance.

The task sets the boundary

A technician needs to view sanitized logs for three hours. Local policy requires a named identity, MFA, owner approval, and expiry. Approval is still pending; no write task is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The requested permission is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. read-only

Viewing the supplied logs does not require writing or administrative permissions.

2. Reviewer: "The requested duration is ___ hours."

Answer and explanation

B. three

The briefing explicitly limits the diagnostic task to three hours.

3. Coordinator: "The identity must be ___."

Answer and explanation

A. named

The stated local policy requires an individually identified account.

4. Reviewer: "Owner approval remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. pending

The supplied facts say approval has not yet been granted.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 04: Identity, Access, and Least Privilege
Original fictional case: Vendor coordinator Eva requests a shared administrator account so two technicians can diagnose a service issue. Access reviewer Rashid confirms that the supplied task needs only read access to sanitized diagnostic logs for two hours. Their local policy requires named identities, multifactor authentication, owner approval, and recorded expiry. No approval has been granted. The proposed alternative gives each technician only the required log access. Any later write task needs a separate assessed request; no broader privilege is justified by the present facts.
Language workshop: Support needs logs, not a shared administrator
Communication goal: Negotiate a specific access arrangement using identity, task, permission, duration, approval, and accountability.
Language guidance: Authentication checks identity; authorization controls permitted actions. Multifactor authentication does not by itself justify administrator rights. Sanitization also does not remove the stated approval, identity, and expiry requirements.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "We need to enable the diagnostic work." / "Which action requires administrator rights?" / "The task only needs sanitized diagnostic logs." / "Read-only access supports that task." / "Each technician needs a named account." / "Multifactor authentication is required." / "The requested access lasts two hours." / "The service owner has not approved it yet." / "We need to associate actions with each technician." / "This request does not justify permanent privilege." / "Record when the access ends." / "A later write task needs a new assessed request." / "The restriction follows the task, not a judgment about the vendor." / "Please include the task, identities, and duration." / "Verify that access ends as approved." / "Submit named, read-only, time-bound access for owner review."
Vocabulary: identity and access management: Processes for managing identities and their permissions. / least privilege: Limiting access to what the authorized task requires. / named account: An account associated with an identified individual. / shared account: An account used by more than one individual. / privileged access: Permission to perform elevated system actions. / read-only access: Permission to view data without changing it. / entitlement: A specific right or permission assigned to an identity. / role-based access: Permissions assigned through defined job or task roles. / multifactor authentication: Verification using more than one distinct factor type. / single sign-on: Access to multiple services through a shared sign-in arrangement. / federation: Trust between identity systems to support identity assertions. / provisioning: Creating or enabling an identity or its access. / deprovisioning: Removing or disabling access that is no longer needed. / access review: Evaluation of whether granted rights remain appropriate. / time-bound access: Permission with a defined end time or duration. / expiry: The point at which a permission or approval ends. / just-in-time access: Access activated only for an approved period of need. / standing privilege: Elevated access retained beyond a particular active task. / session record: Information documenting a particular access session. / accountability: Ability to associate actions with responsible identities. / separation of duties: Dividing sensitive responsibilities to reduce conflicting control. / break-glass access: Controlled emergency access outside the normal route. / service account: An identity used by a service or automated process. / sanitized logs: Diagnostic records with restricted details removed or protected.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Contrast read-only access with administrator rights. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress pending approval and the recorded expiry. Check the transfer. Read the three-hour proposal as a request, not an approval.
Partner: Eva: Vendor coordinator / Rashid: Access reviewer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A technician needs to view sanitized logs for three hours. Local policy requires a named identity, MFA, owner approval, and expiry. Approval is still pending; no write task is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Eva: I will put the identities, read-only log scope, multifactor requirement, two-hour duration, and owner decision in the request. What should I say about the end?
Rashid: Record the expiry and confirm how it will be enforced. The access should not remain active simply because everyone assumes somebody else will remove it later.
Eva: Then I will send the bounded proposal for owner review and avoid promising administrator access. Any changed task will return with a specific justification.
Rashid: Good. Verify deprovisioning when the approved window ends. We can enable the diagnostic work while keeping permission, duration, approval, and individual responsibility explicit.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cybersecurity English
Professional audience: security analysts, SOC staff, incident responders, security engineers, GRC specialists, identity teams, vulnerability managers, and security leaders
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not a security certification, incident-response playbook, legal opinion, or authorization to test systems. Follow the responsible team, approved procedures, evidence-handling rules, and applicable requirements. All organizations, incidents, figures, priorities, and control criteria in the cases are invented.
Lesson 04: Identity, Access, and Least Privilege
Original fictional case: Vendor coordinator Eva requests a shared administrator account so two technicians can diagnose a service issue. Access reviewer Rashid confirms that the supplied task needs only read access to sanitized diagnostic logs for two hours. Their local policy requires named identities, multifactor authentication, owner approval, and recorded expiry. No approval has been granted. The proposed alternative gives each technician only the required log access. Any later write task needs a separate assessed request; no broader privilege is justified by the present facts.
Language workshop: Support needs logs, not a shared administrator
Communication goal: Negotiate a specific access arrangement using identity, task, permission, duration, approval, and accountability.
Language guidance: Authentication checks identity; authorization controls permitted actions. Multifactor authentication does not by itself justify administrator rights. Sanitization also does not remove the stated approval, identity, and expiry requirements.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "We need to enable the diagnostic work." / "Which action requires administrator rights?" / "The task only needs sanitized diagnostic logs." / "Read-only access supports that task." / "Each technician needs a named account." / "Multifactor authentication is required." / "The requested access lasts two hours." / "The service owner has not approved it yet." / "We need to associate actions with each technician." / "This request does not justify permanent privilege." / "Record when the access ends." / "A later write task needs a new assessed request." / "The restriction follows the task, not a judgment about the vendor." / "Please include the task, identities, and duration." / "Verify that access ends as approved." / "Submit named, read-only, time-bound access for owner review."
Vocabulary: identity and access management: Processes for managing identities and their permissions. / least privilege: Limiting access to what the authorized task requires. / named account: An account associated with an identified individual. / shared account: An account used by more than one individual. / privileged access: Permission to perform elevated system actions. / read-only access: Permission to view data without changing it. / entitlement: A specific right or permission assigned to an identity. / role-based access: Permissions assigned through defined job or task roles. / multifactor authentication: Verification using more than one distinct factor type. / single sign-on: Access to multiple services through a shared sign-in arrangement. / federation: Trust between identity systems to support identity assertions. / provisioning: Creating or enabling an identity or its access. / deprovisioning: Removing or disabling access that is no longer needed. / access review: Evaluation of whether granted rights remain appropriate. / time-bound access: Permission with a defined end time or duration. / expiry: The point at which a permission or approval ends. / just-in-time access: Access activated only for an approved period of need. / standing privilege: Elevated access retained beyond a particular active task. / session record: Information documenting a particular access session. / accountability: Ability to associate actions with responsible identities. / separation of duties: Dividing sensitive responsibilities to reduce conflicting control. / break-glass access: Controlled emergency access outside the normal route. / service account: An identity used by a service or automated process. / sanitized logs: Diagnostic records with restricted details removed or protected.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Contrast read-only access with administrator rights. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress pending approval and the recorded expiry. Check the transfer. Read the three-hour proposal as a request, not an approval.
Partner: Eva: Vendor coordinator / Rashid: Access reviewer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A technician needs to view sanitized logs for three hours. Local policy requires a named identity, MFA, owner approval, and expiry. Approval is still pending; no write task is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Eva: I will put the identities, read-only log scope, multifactor requirement, two-hour duration, and owner decision in the request. What should I say about the end?
Rashid: Record the expiry and confirm how it will be enforced. The access should not remain active simply because everyone assumes somebody else will remove it later.
Eva: Then I will send the bounded proposal for owner review and avoid promising administrator access. Any changed task will return with a specific justification.
Rashid: Good. Verify deprovisioning when the approved window ends. We can enable the diagnostic work while keeping permission, duration, approval, and individual responsibility explicit.
END REFERENCE
05Threat Modeling and Secure DesignA sign-in check does not decide document access+

Your goal

Trace a data flow, identify a trust boundary, and turn a specific abuse case into a testable design requirement.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A sign-in check does not decide document access

Architect Noor and security engineer Daniel review a fictional document portal. A signed-in browser sends a document ID to a service, which retrieves a file from storage. The draft checks the user at sign-in but does not specify a server-side check that the user may access the requested document. Customers must see only their own organization-owned documents. The review has no implemented-system test result or confirmed breach. Daniel proposes a per-request authorization check and an approved test using separate fictional tenant accounts.

Noor: Application architect / Daniel: Security engineer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the supplied draft already check?

Answer and explanation

D. The user at sign-in

The draft describes a sign-in check but lacks the separate per-document authorization decision.

2. What security requirement is supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. Customers may see only their own organization-owned documents.

The briefing explicitly defines the organization-level document-access boundary.

3. What is not established?

Answer and explanation

A. A confirmed breach or implemented-system test result

The discussion concerns the draft design, not an already proven live exploit.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Identity and object permission are different decisions. A legitimate user may still request a document belonging to another organization. The design must specify where that permission is enforced, not merely that sign-in exists.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
threat model
A structured account of relevant threats and responses.review the threat model
data flow
Movement of information between system elements.trace the data flow
trust boundary
A point where assumptions about control or trust change.identify a trust boundary
external entity
An actor or system outside the modeled component.identify external entities
process
A component that transforms or acts on data.model the service process
data store
A location where information is retained.identify data stores
asset
Something of value requiring protection.identify sensitive assets
entry point
A place where data or interaction enters a system.review entry points
abuse case
A scenario describing misuse of intended functionality.define an abuse case
security requirement
A checkable condition protecting a security objective.specify a security requirement
authentication check
A check of the claimed identity.perform an authentication check
authorization check
A check of permission for a requested action.enforce an authorization check
object-level authorization
Permission enforcement for a specific requested object.verify object-level authorization
tenant
A customer or organization sharing a service.isolate tenant data
tenant isolation
Separation preventing unauthorized cross-tenant access.test tenant isolation
server-side enforcement
A decision enforced by the service rather than the client.require server-side enforcement
untrusted input
Data not accepted as authoritative without validation.handle untrusted input
document identifier
A value used to refer to a specific document.validate the document identifier
STRIDE
Spoofing, tampering, repudiation, information disclosure, denial of service, elevation of privilege.apply STRIDE categories
spoofing
Misrepresenting an identity or source.assess spoofing threats
tampering
Unauthorized modification of data or system state.prevent data tampering
information disclosure
Exposure of information to an unauthorized party.prevent information disclosure
denial of service
Disruption of access to a service or resource.assess denial-of-service risk
elevation of privilege
Gaining rights beyond the permitted level.prevent elevation of privilege

This is a design review, not proof of a breach. The proposed test belongs in an approved environment with controlled test accounts and data. A passing identity check alone does not satisfy the document-access requirement.

C Notice the language

Challenge the assumption, not the designer

A useful design challenge names the specific request, boundary, and missing decision. It does not accuse the architect of causing a breach. Distinguish a documented design gap from an observed production defect while making the next requirement concrete.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Start with the request
"The browser sends a document identifier to the service."
Trace the next step
"The service retrieves the file from storage."
Name the boundary
"The client-provided identifier crosses a trust boundary."
Acknowledge the existing check
"The draft checks identity at sign-in."
Identify the missing decision
"It does not specify permission for this document."
State the protected outcome
"Customers must see only their own organization's documents."
Keep the claim bounded
"We have a design gap, not a confirmed breach."
Make the abuse case concrete
"A signed-in user requests another tenant's document."
Place enforcement
"The permission check belongs on the server for each request."
Reject client trust
"A browser-supplied identifier is not authorization."
Define the denied case
"The service must deny access when the document is outside the user's permitted scope."
Preserve legitimate work
"The allowed same-tenant request must still work."
Use controlled testing
"Use approved test accounts and fictional documents."
Assign the requirement
"Add the access condition to the design and tests."
Separate categories
"This case concerns unauthorized disclosure of document content."
Close the review
"Record the requirement, its owner, and the validation evidence needed."
6 language and precision notes
Authenticated
Does not mean authorized for every object.
Missing in the draft
Not the same as proven absent in the implementation.
Trust
State which input or claim is being relied upon.
Per request
Check the action each time under the intended rule.
Deny
Specify the condition and observable protected outcome.
Test
Must have approved scope, controlled data, and a clear expected result.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which requirement closes the identified design gap?

Answer and explanation

A. Enforce server-side permission for each requested document.

The missing decision concerns permission for the requested object, not only identity or interface presentation.

2. Which pair best tests the stated boundary?

Answer and explanation

B. An authorized same-tenant request and a denied cross-tenant request using controlled data

The pair checks both intended access and the protected boundary within authorized test scope.

3. Which statement overclaims the review evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. A customer breach has definitely occurred.

The briefing explicitly supplies no confirmed breach or implemented-system test result.

4. Why is a browser-supplied ID not enough?

Answer and explanation

C. A value naming an object does not establish permission to access it.

Identifying a document and authorizing access to it are separate functions.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

A sign-in check does not decide document access

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Noor: Application architect / Daniel: Security engineer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • trust boundary
  • tenant isolation
  • authorization check
  • security requirement
  • abuse case
  • data flow
  • information disclosure
  • threat model
  • object-level authorization
  • server-side enforcement
  1. Noor
    The diagram lists the browser, service, and storage. We require sign-in before the document page, so I thought the main access question was covered.
  2. Daniel
    Let us trace the 1. A browser sends a document identifier, the service processes it, and storage returns content. Where is permission for that particular document checked?
  3. Noor
    The draft does not say. It describes checking the user at sign-in, then retrieving the requested file. Our customers should see only their own organization's documents.
  4. Daniel
    That leaves an 2 unspecified. We know who signed in, but we still need the service to decide whether that user may access the requested object.
  5. Noor
    So the gap is not simply that somebody could be anonymous. It also concerns a legitimate user asking for a document outside their organization.
  6. Daniel
    Exactly. That is the 3 we should record. It makes the missing decision concrete without claiming that the implemented system has already leaked anything or that a breach occurred.
  7. Noor
    Could we just hide documents from other organizations in the browser? Or does the service need to enforce the same boundary independently?
  8. Daniel
    The client crosses a 4. A page convention does not give the server a dependable authorization decision. We need enforcement where the service controls access to the content.
  9. Noor
    Then the requirement should say the service checks permission on every request, using the authenticated user's allowed organization scope and the requested document.
  10. Daniel
    Yes, that is 5. The service must deny access when the document is outside that permitted scope, while keeping the legitimate same-tenant request working.
  11. Noor
    I will put both outcomes into the design. Otherwise someone could call the issue fixed by denying every request and breaking the ordinary feature.
  12. Daniel
    Good. A useful 6 is checkable and preserves intended use. We should identify the denied case and the allowed case instead of saying only make document access secure.
  13. Noor
    For validation, we can use two fictional organizations with controlled test accounts and documents in the approved environment. No live customer exploration is needed.
  14. Daniel
    That supports a 7 check within an authorized scope. Record which account owns which document, the requested action, and the expected result before interpreting the observed outcome.
  15. Noor
    What label goes in the review: confirmed vulnerability or design gap? We have a draft in front of us, not an implementation test.
  16. Daniel
    Keep the 8 precise: documented design gap, proposed requirement, validation pending. We should not manufacture certainty, but the lack of a live failure does not erase the gap.
  17. Noor
    I will assign the requirement to the service owner and include the browser input, storage ownership, and access-decision location in the revised diagram.
  18. Daniel
    Make 9 explicit. The next reviewer should not have to guess whether the note describes a user-interface preference or an actual control over returned content.
  19. Noor
    The review record will distinguish the existing sign-in check, missing per-document rule, proposed controlled tests, and evidence still needed before we close the action.
  20. Daniel
    That is clear. The relevant harm here is 10, and the response is a defined access decision. We have moved from a technology list to a reviewable requirement without overstating the evidence.

Answers and explanations
  1. data flow Turn 2. The data flow shows how the identifier moves from the browser through the service to storage.
  2. authorization check Turn 4. The authorization check decides permission for the requested document, distinct from verifying identity at sign-in.
  3. abuse case Turn 6. The abuse case describes a signed-in user attempting access outside the intended document boundary.
  4. trust boundary Turn 8. The trust boundary separates client-controlled input from the service's responsibility to enforce access decisions.
  5. object-level authorization Turn 10. Object-level authorization applies the permission rule to each specific requested document rather than to sign-in alone.
  6. security requirement Turn 12. A security requirement states a checkable protected outcome while preserving the intended authorized behavior.
  7. tenant isolation Turn 14. Tenant isolation concerns preventing unauthorized access between customer organizations sharing the service.
  8. threat model Turn 16. The threat model records the design-level threat and response without converting an untested design gap into a confirmed breach.
  9. server-side enforcement Turn 18. Server-side enforcement places the permission decision in the controlled service rather than relying on browser behavior.
  10. information disclosure Turn 20. Information disclosure is unauthorized exposure of document content, the specific harm addressed by this access requirement.
