Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

List the data flows, not only technologies

Fictional threat-model review using the lesson case: a diagram lists technologies but does not identify data flows or trust boundaries.

  1. Security designer: The diagram lists technologies. Could you show what data moves between them and who controls each endpoint?
  2. Application architect: Those flows aren't drawn yet. I assumed naming the database and web service was enough.
  3. Security designer: For this threat model, we need to understand where data crosses a trust boundary.
  4. Application architect: You mean where one component cannot simply assume another source is authorized or trustworthy?
  5. Security designer: Yes. Please identify the actual sources, destinations, and checks rather than leave that relationship implicit.
  6. Application architect: Should we invent likely flows from a typical architecture while the owner is unavailable?
  7. Security designer: No. Mark missing information and ask the owner; guessed flows could misdirect the review.
  8. Application architect: I'll bring the verified flow descriptions and identify which interactions remain undocumented.
  9. Security designer: Then we can discuss relevant abuse cases and attack surface without pretending the technology list is complete.
  10. Application architect: Agreed. The next review will start from confirmed interactions, with unknown boundaries clearly labeled.
Who can send the webhook?

Fictional secure-design discussion: a service receives partner webhooks, but sender verification and receiving actions are undocumented.

  1. Threat-model facilitator: Who can send a webhook to this endpoint, and what action does a received message trigger?
  2. Service developer: The design says partner webhook, but it doesn't document sender verification or the resulting action.
  3. Threat-model facilitator: Then partner is an intended source, not evidence that the service verifies that source.
  4. Service developer: Could we mark this trust boundary as protected because the endpoint name isn't advertised publicly?
  5. Threat-model facilitator: No. We need the actual authorization and validation design, not an assumption based on obscurity.
  6. Service developer: I'll ask the integration owner for the data flow and the checks before any action occurs.
  7. Threat-model facilitator: Please also clarify what data is stored and which downstream component receives it.
  8. Service developer: That will let us discuss an abuse case without constructing one from missing architecture details.
  9. Threat-model facilitator: Exactly. We're identifying questions about the attack surface, not claiming a confirmed exploitable vulnerability.
  10. Service developer: I'll return with verified design details and keep the unknown checks explicit until then.
An internal upload still crosses a boundary

Fictional document-tool design: employees upload files to a shared processing service; file origins and processing permissions have not been mapped.

  1. Application owner: Only employees use the upload tool. Do we still need to describe a trust boundary?
  2. Security architect: Yes. Could you clarify where files originate and what the processing service can do with them?
  3. Application owner: We haven't mapped that. Internal users may upload files obtained elsewhere, but the actual sources are unrecorded.
  4. Security architect: Then internal access doesn't establish every file's trustworthiness or the appropriate processing permissions.
  5. Application owner: Should the threat model distinguish user authorization from the content being processed?
  6. Security architect: Exactly. They are related but separate questions, and we need the real data flow for both.
  7. Application owner: I'll ask the service owner which storage and downstream operations are involved before proposing controls.
  8. Security architect: Please keep the abuse-case discussion at the design-review level and identify missing facts rather than invent them.
  9. Application owner: I'll bring a verified flow description with uncertain origins and permissions clearly marked.
  10. Security architect: Then we can assess the relevant attack surface using the actual system boundaries.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Cybersecurity English
Professional audience: security analysts, SOC staff, incident responders, security engineers, GRC specialists, identity teams, vulnerability managers, and security leaders
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not a security certification, incident-response playbook, legal opinion, or authorization to test systems. Follow the responsible team, approved procedures, evidence-handling rules, and applicable requirements. All organizations, incidents, figures, priorities, and control criteria in the cases are invented.
Lesson 05: Threat Modeling and Secure Design
Original fictional case: Architect Noor and security engineer Daniel review a fictional document portal. A signed-in browser sends a document ID to a service, which retrieves a file from storage. The draft checks the user at sign-in but does not specify a server-side check that the user may access the requested document. Customers must see only their own organization-owned documents. The review has no implemented-system test result or confirmed breach. Daniel proposes a per-request authorization check and an approved test using separate fictional tenant accounts.
Language workshop: A sign-in check does not decide document access
Communication goal: Trace a data flow, identify a trust boundary, and turn a specific abuse case into a testable design requirement.
Language guidance: Identity and object permission are different decisions. A legitimate user may still request a document belonging to another organization. The design must specify where that permission is enforced, not merely that sign-in exists.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The browser sends a document identifier to the service." / "The service retrieves the file from storage." / "The client-provided identifier crosses a trust boundary." / "The draft checks identity at sign-in." / "It does not specify permission for this document." / "Customers must see only their own organization's documents." / "We have a design gap, not a confirmed breach." / "A signed-in user requests another tenant's document." / "The permission check belongs on the server for each request." / "A browser-supplied identifier is not authorization." / "The service must deny access when the document is outside the user's permitted scope." / "The allowed same-tenant request must still work." / "Use approved test accounts and fictional documents." / "Add the access condition to the design and tests." / "This case concerns unauthorized disclosure of document content." / "Record the requirement, its owner, and the validation evidence needed."
Vocabulary: threat model: A structured account of relevant threats and responses. / data flow: Movement of information between system elements. / trust boundary: A point where assumptions about control or trust change. / external entity: An actor or system outside the modeled component. / process: A component that transforms or acts on data. / data store: A location where information is retained. / asset: Something of value requiring protection. / entry point: A place where data or interaction enters a system. / abuse case: A scenario describing misuse of intended functionality. / security requirement: A checkable condition protecting a security objective. / authentication check: A check of the claimed identity. / authorization check: A check of permission for a requested action. / object-level authorization: Permission enforcement for a specific requested object. / tenant: A customer or organization sharing a service. / tenant isolation: Separation preventing unauthorized cross-tenant access. / server-side enforcement: A decision enforced by the service rather than the client. / untrusted input: Data not accepted as authoritative without validation. / document identifier: A value used to refer to a specific document. / STRIDE: Spoofing, tampering, repudiation, information disclosure, denial of service, elevation of privilege. / spoofing: Misrepresenting an identity or source. / tampering: Unauthorized modification of data or system state. / information disclosure: Exposure of information to an unauthorized party. / denial of service: Disruption of access to a service or resource. / elevation of privilege: Gaining rights beyond the permitted level.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress identity and document permission as separate decisions. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep design evidence distinct from an implementation test. Check the transfer. Read identity, authorization, and server as different parts of the control.
Partner: Noor: Application architect / Daniel: Security engineer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A design checks identity at sign-in but omits permission checks for individual invoices. Customers may access only their own invoices. No implementation test has run. The proposed control belongs in the service.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Noor: I will assign the requirement to the service owner and include the browser input, storage ownership, and access-decision location in the revised diagram.
Daniel: Make server-side enforcement explicit. The next reviewer should not have to guess whether the note describes a user-interface preference or an actual control over returned content.
Noor: The review record will distinguish the existing sign-in check, missing per-document rule, proposed controlled tests, and evidence still needed before we close the action.
Daniel: That is clear. The relevant harm here is information disclosure, and the response is a defined access decision. We have moved from a technology list to a reviewable requirement without overstating the evidence.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Governance, Risk, and ComplianceA written policy does not close the control finding+

Your goal

Describe audit evidence, distinguish missing proof from proven failure, and state the actual closure conditions.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A written policy does not close the control finding

Control owner Rosa asks auditor Amir to close a leaver-access finding because a new policy was approved. The local control requires access removal within 24 hours of departure. In a five-person sample, three records prove timely removal; two lack removal timestamps. The agreed closure criteria require evidence for all five sampled cases and owner review. That review is not recorded. Amir must distinguish a documentation gap from proof that the two removals were late, while keeping the finding open.

Rosa: Control owner / Amir: Internal auditor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many sampled records prove timely removal?

Answer and explanation

C. Three of five

Three records contain evidence of removal within the stated twenty-four-hour limit.

2. What do the other two records lack?

Answer and explanation

D. Removal timestamps

The briefing identifies missing timestamps rather than confirmed late removals.

3. Can the finding close under the supplied criteria?

Answer and explanation

B. No; evidence and owner review are incomplete.

The stated closure conditions require all five cases to be evidenced and owner review recorded.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Three of five is 60% of the sample with documented timely removal. It is not a demonstrated 60% organization-wide effectiveness rate. The two missing timestamps do not establish whether those removals were timely or late.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
governance
Arrangements for directing decisions, responsibilities, and oversight.strengthen security governance
control objective
The outcome a control is intended to achieve.define the control objective
control design
The planned way a control is meant to work.assess control design
operating effectiveness
Whether a control works as intended over the assessed scope.test operating effectiveness
policy
An approved statement of required principles or rules.approve an access policy
procedure
Defined steps for carrying out a process.maintain the removal procedure
control owner
The role accountable for a defined control.identify the control owner
evidence request
A specified request for records supporting an assessment.issue an evidence request
audit trail
Records allowing actions and decisions to be traced.preserve the audit trail
sample
A selected subset examined in an assessment.define the audit sample
population
The full set from which assessment items are selected.establish the population
sampling limitation
A restriction on what a sample supports about the wider set.disclose sampling limitations
exception
A departure from a defined requirement or expected condition.investigate an exception
evidence gap
Missing support needed to substantiate a conclusion.resolve an evidence gap
finding
A documented assessment issue supported by stated criteria and evidence.track an audit finding
remediation plan
Agreed work to address an identified finding.review the remediation plan
closure criterion
A stated condition required to close an action.satisfy closure criteria
attestation
A formal statement affirming a specified matter.obtain an owner attestation
independent review
Assessment by a sufficiently separate reviewer.conduct an independent review
retention period
The required duration for keeping records.apply the retention period
control frequency
How often a control is intended to operate.specify control frequency
leaver process
Handling required when a person leaves an organization.verify the leaver process
access removal
Disabling or withdrawing previously granted permissions.evidence timely access removal
residual risk
Risk remaining after the relevant controls or treatment.assess residual risk

Policy approval supports the existence of a rule. Operating evidence supports what happened in practice. The supplied closure conditions require all five sampled cases and recorded owner review, so the finding remains open.

C Notice the language

Make the evidence request specific

A challenge to closure need not dismiss the work already completed. Acknowledge the approved policy, then name the missing records and review. Avoid both extremes: treating paperwork as operating proof or calling every undocumented action a proven late removal.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge progress
"The new policy has been approved."
Separate design from operation
"That does not yet establish operating effectiveness."
State the criterion
"Access removal is required within 24 hours."
Give the supported count
"Three of five records prove timely removal."
Name the missing evidence
"Two records lack removal timestamps."
Avoid a false failure claim
"We cannot determine timeliness for those two from these records."
Limit the percentage
"Sixty percent of this sample has documented timely removal."
Preserve the closure rule
"The agreed criteria require evidence for all five cases."
Ask for the review
"Please provide the recorded owner review."
Avoid retroactive invention
"Do not create a timestamp as though it were an original record."
Request traceable support
"Use the relevant system evidence and identify its source."
Distinguish assurance
"An assurance statement is not a substitute for the required record."
Keep the status accurate
"The finding remains open pending the missing evidence."
Bound the conclusion
"This sample does not establish the result for the entire population."
Assign the next step
"The control owner will gather the missing records."
Close the discussion
"We will reassess closure against the same agreed criteria."
6 language and precision notes
Written
Establishes a document exists, not that people followed it.
Timely
Needs both a defined deadline and relevant event evidence.
Undocumented
Does not automatically mean not done.
Sample
State its size and avoid unwarranted population claims.
Closed
A conclusion under stated criteria, not a reward for effort.
Attest
Make clear what is affirmed and what evidence remains required.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Three timely removals are evidenced; two are unverified; owner review is missing.

The correct summary keeps documented timeliness, missing evidence, and missing review separate.

2. What is 3 divided by 5 in this case?

Answer and explanation

D. 60% of the sample with documented timely removal

The calculation applies only to the supplied five-item sample and evidence status.

3. Which evidence request is appropriately specific?

Answer and explanation

C. Provide source records for the two removal times and the owner review.

The request directly addresses the missing closure evidence without inventing historical facts.

4. Why should the finding remain open?

Answer and explanation

D. The agreed evidence and review conditions are not met.

Closure follows the supplied criteria, not personal approval or unsupported accusations.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

A written policy does not close the control finding

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Control owner / Amir: Internal auditor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • attestation
  • control owner
  • control design
  • remediation plan
  • audit trail
  • closure criteria
  • evidence gap
  • sample
  • operating effectiveness
  • sampling limitation
  1. Rosa
    The new leaver policy is approved. It requires removal within twenty-four hours, and I would like the audit action marked closed before the committee meeting.
  2. Amir
    Policy approval is useful progress on 1, but closure also requires evidence that the control operated. Let us compare the supplied records with the criteria we agreed.
  3. Rosa
    We selected five leavers. Three records include departure and removal times within the required period. The other two do not include removal timestamps.
  4. Amir
    Then 2 is not established for all five. We should not describe the policy document as if it were evidence of each actual removal.
  5. Rosa
    Could I say the control was sixty percent effective? Three out of five is sixty percent, and that seems more concise for the committee.
  6. Amir
    Report the 3 accurately: sixty percent has documented timely removal. That does not establish organization-wide effectiveness, and it does not prove the other two were late.
  7. Rosa
    I agree about not calling them late without evidence. The help desk thinks the removals happened, but we do not yet have the original supporting records.
  8. Amir
    That is an 4. Request the relevant system records and identify their source. A recollection can guide the search, but it should not become an invented historical timestamp.
  9. Rosa
    The closure note says evidence for every sampled case and owner review. I have not completed a recorded review because the two records are still missing.
  10. Amir
    Those are the 5 we must use. The finding remains open until the missing support and required review are addressed, rather than closing merely because the policy now exists.
  11. Rosa
    The committee will ask why it is still open. Can we show the approved policy and three supported cases without implying the remaining work is done?
  12. Amir
    Show that in the 6. Completed work and an open finding can coexist. A progress update should say what changed and what still prevents the agreed closure.
  13. Rosa
    I will assign the record retrieval and keep the committee wording bounded. For the missing cases, I will use timeliness unverified rather than late.
  14. Amir
    That preserves the 7. We need a traceable account of the assessment, including any later records or corrections, not a headline that hides unresolved support.
  15. Rosa
    Could I sign a statement about our normal process? I can describe what the team does, but I cannot supply those two times yet.
  16. Amir
    An 8 may be relevant context, but it does not replace the specific records required here. We should not silently change the closure standard at the end.
  17. Rosa
    Understood. I will separate approval of the policy, evidence for three timely removals, two unverified cases, and the owner review still to be recorded.
  18. Amir
    Also state the 9. We examined five records; we have not established the result for every leaver. The percentage needs both its denominator and its evidence meaning.
  19. Rosa
    Then I can give the committee a truthful progress report and a clear remaining action, without claiming that missing documentation proves misconduct or lateness.
  20. Amir
    Exactly. The 10 can bring the missing evidence for reassessment. We will evaluate closure against the same criteria and record the conclusion the completed support actually justifies.

Answers and explanations
  1. control design Turn 2. Control design concerns the planned rule and process, which policy approval supports without proving operation.
  2. operating effectiveness Turn 4. Operating effectiveness concerns actual performance; the incomplete records do not establish it for every sampled case.
  3. sample Turn 6. The sample is the five examined cases, so the percentage must be tied to those records and their evidence status.
  4. evidence gap Turn 8. The evidence gap is the absence of timestamps needed to determine timeliness, not proof of a late action.
  5. closure criteria Turn 10. The closure criteria explicitly require all five cases to be evidenced and owner review recorded.
  6. remediation plan Turn 12. The remediation plan can record completed policy work and the remaining evidence and review tasks without falsely closing the finding.
  7. audit trail Turn 14. The audit trail records what the evidence shows and how later conclusions are reached without overwriting the uncertainty.
  8. attestation Turn 16. An attestation is a formal statement, but it does not replace the source evidence expressly required by these closure criteria.
  9. sampling limitation Turn 18. The sampling limitation prevents the five reviewed cases from being presented as proof about every leaver in the organization.
  10. control owner Turn 20. The control owner is responsible for gathering the remaining support and completing the required review in this case.

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Written policy, missing operating evidence

Fictional audit review using the lesson case: an action is marked closed because a policy exists, but evidence that the control operates is missing.

  1. Audit coordinator: The action is marked closed because the policy was written. Where is evidence that the control operates?
  2. Control owner: We have the approved text, but we haven't supplied operating records for the review period.
  3. Audit coordinator: Then policy completion is established, while control-operation evidence remains pending. Those statuses should be separate.
  4. Control owner: Does that mean the control definitely failed, or just that we haven't demonstrated its operation?
  5. Audit coordinator: Here the evidence is missing. We shouldn't turn that gap into either verified effectiveness or confirmed failure.
  6. Control owner: I'll reopen the evidence item and identify which records the reviewer requires for assessment.
  7. Audit coordinator: Please use the agreed audit process and preserve the policy version alongside the operating evidence.
  8. Control owner: Could a risk-acceptance note replace the missing records and let us close remediation automatically?
  9. Audit coordinator: Not automatically. Any acceptance requires the appropriate authority and does not invent evidence of operation.
  10. Control owner: Understood. I'll report policy complete, evidence pending, and closure awaiting the responsible review.
A screenshot without a review period

Fictional control assessment: a current settings screenshot is available, but the audit asks about operation over the previous quarter.

  1. Control owner: I attached today's settings screenshot. Can the quarterly operating-evidence request now be closed?
  2. Assurance analyst: The screenshot shows a current configuration, not necessarily how the control operated throughout the requested quarter.
  3. Control owner: So the artifact is useful, but it doesn't cover the entire evidence period by itself.
  4. Assurance analyst: Correct. We need to confirm what records support that period under the agreed assessment scope.
  5. Control owner: Would saying the configuration probably stayed unchanged be enough to bridge the missing months?
  6. Assurance analyst: No. That is an assumption, not period-specific audit evidence. Keep the coverage gap visible.
  7. Control owner: I'll ask the record owner for available historical evidence and identify any unrecoverable gaps.
  8. Assurance analyst: Please keep today's screenshot as current-state evidence rather than discarding it or mislabeling its date.
  9. Control owner: I'll update the remediation status to show evidence collection incomplete, without claiming the control failed.
  10. Assurance analyst: Then the reviewer can assess actual coverage and decide what remains necessary for closure.
Risk acceptance is requested, not approved

Fictional governance update: remediation is deferred in a proposal, and a risk-acceptance request has been submitted but not approved.

  1. Project lead: We submitted the risk-acceptance request. Can I say the remaining risk is formally accepted?
  2. Risk analyst: Not yet. Submission is complete, but the authorized decision is still pending.
  3. Project lead: The proposal explains why remediation would take longer. Doesn't that establish a valid deferral?
  4. Risk analyst: It provides a rationale for review, not approval by itself. The decision owner must assess it.
  5. Project lead: Then our status should distinguish the remediation proposal from the governance decision it needs.
  6. Risk analyst: Exactly. Also keep existing controls and evidence gaps visible so the reviewer sees the current position.
  7. Project lead: Could I mark the action closed while leaving a note that acceptance is awaiting signature?
  8. Risk analyst: That would imply closure before the required review. Use the actual pending status instead.
  9. Project lead: I'll correct the update and ask the decision owner for a review checkpoint, not a guaranteed approval.
  10. Risk analyst: Good. The report will reflect completed submission and unresolved acceptance without conflating the two.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A written policy does not close the control finding
  1. Read turns 1-10. Contrast three supported cases with two unverified cases.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress sample limits and the required owner review.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Missing proof is not a proven delay

A four-case access-removal sample has three documented timely removals and one missing timestamp. Closure requires evidence for all four and an owner review. Neither the missing evidence nor the review is complete.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Auditor: "For the fourth case, we still need the removal ___."

Answer and explanation

C. timestamp

The missing removal timestamp is the source evidence needed to compare the action with the deadline.

2. Owner: "Without it, I cannot verify that case's ___."

Answer and explanation

D. timeliness

Timeliness is unresolved; the missing record does not prove either timely or late removal.

3. Auditor: "We also need the recorded owner ___."

Answer and explanation

B. review

The local closure criteria separately require an owner review that has not been completed.

4. Owner: "Keep the finding open until the evidence and review meet our conditions for ___."

Answer and explanation

B. closure

Closure depends on both outstanding requirements, not merely the existence of the approved policy.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Governance, Risk, and Compliance
Original fictional case: Control owner Rosa asks auditor Amir to close a leaver-access finding because a new policy was approved. The local control requires access removal within 24 hours of departure. In a five-person sample, three records prove timely removal; two lack removal timestamps. The agreed closure criteria require evidence for all five sampled cases and owner review. That review is not recorded. Amir must distinguish a documentation gap from proof that the two removals were late, while keeping the finding open.
Language workshop: A written policy does not close the control finding
Communication goal: Describe audit evidence, distinguish missing proof from proven failure, and state the actual closure conditions.
Language guidance: Three of five is 60% of the sample with documented timely removal. It is not a demonstrated 60% organization-wide effectiveness rate. The two missing timestamps do not establish whether those removals were timely or late.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The new policy has been approved." / "That does not yet establish operating effectiveness." / "Access removal is required within 24 hours." / "Three of five records prove timely removal." / "Two records lack removal timestamps." / "We cannot determine timeliness for those two from these records." / "Sixty percent of this sample has documented timely removal." / "The agreed criteria require evidence for all five cases." / "Please provide the recorded owner review." / "Do not create a timestamp as though it were an original record." / "Use the relevant system evidence and identify its source." / "An assurance statement is not a substitute for the required record." / "The finding remains open pending the missing evidence." / "This sample does not establish the result for the entire population." / "The control owner will gather the missing records." / "We will reassess closure against the same agreed criteria."
Vocabulary: governance: Arrangements for directing decisions, responsibilities, and oversight. / control objective: The outcome a control is intended to achieve. / control design: The planned way a control is meant to work. / operating effectiveness: Whether a control works as intended over the assessed scope. / policy: An approved statement of required principles or rules. / procedure: Defined steps for carrying out a process. / control owner: The role accountable for a defined control. / evidence request: A specified request for records supporting an assessment. / audit trail: Records allowing actions and decisions to be traced. / sample: A selected subset examined in an assessment. / population: The full set from which assessment items are selected. / sampling limitation: A restriction on what a sample supports about the wider set. / exception: A departure from a defined requirement or expected condition. / evidence gap: Missing support needed to substantiate a conclusion. / finding: A documented assessment issue supported by stated criteria and evidence. / remediation plan: Agreed work to address an identified finding. / closure criterion: A stated condition required to close an action. / attestation: A formal statement affirming a specified matter. / independent review: Assessment by a sufficiently separate reviewer. / retention period: The required duration for keeping records. / control frequency: How often a control is intended to operate. / leaver process: Handling required when a person leaves an organization. / access removal: Disabling or withdrawing previously granted permissions. / residual risk: Risk remaining after the relevant controls or treatment.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Contrast three supported cases with two unverified cases. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress sample limits and the required owner review. Check the transfer. Read the evidence request without calling the missing case late.
Partner: Rosa: Control owner / Amir: Internal auditor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A four-case access-removal sample has three documented timely removals and one missing timestamp. Closure requires evidence for all four and an owner review. Neither the missing evidence nor the review is complete.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Understood. I will separate approval of the policy, evidence for three timely removals, two unverified cases, and the owner review still to be recorded.
Amir: Also state the sampling limitation. We examined five records; we have not established the result for every leaver. The percentage needs both its denominator and its evidence meaning.
Rosa: Then I can give the committee a truthful progress report and a clear remaining action, without claiming that missing documentation proves misconduct or lateness.
Amir: Exactly. The control owner can bring the missing evidence for reassessment. We will evaluate closure against the same criteria and record the conclusion the completed support actually justifies.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Security Awareness and PhishingReport the click, without guessing the consequence+

Your goal

Elicit a precise suspicious-message report, encourage prompt disclosure, and avoid requests for secrets or unsupported reassurance.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Report the click, without guessing the consequence

Employee Ben calls security responder Leila about an email claiming to be from Payroll. He clicked its link at 09:32 UTC and reported it at 09:36. He says he entered no credentials, opened no attachment, and approved no sign-in prompt. He worries that reporting will get him blamed. The message has not been classified, and compromise is not established. The local process routes the original message through the approved reporting tool. Leila asks for factual details, not passwords, and tells Ben not to interact further while the response team assesses it.

Ben: Employee reporter / Leila: Security responder

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did Ben report doing?

Answer and explanation

D. Clicking the link without entering credentials, opening an attachment, or approving a prompt

The briefing gives a link click and explicitly excludes the other reported actions.

2. How long after the click did Ben report it?

Answer and explanation

C. Four minutes

The time from 09:32 to 09:36 UTC is four minutes.

3. What is the message status?

Answer and explanation

C. Not yet classified

The briefing leaves both message classification and compromise unestablished.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

No credentials entered is important reported information, not proof that no risk exists. Preserve what the employee says as a report until corroborated where needed. Message appearance and a familiar display name do not establish authenticity.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
phishing
Deceptive communication intended to induce unsafe disclosure or action.report suspected phishing
social engineering
Manipulating people to gain information or access.recognize social-engineering tactics
pretext
A claimed reason used to justify a request.identify the pretext
impersonation
Pretending to be a trusted person or organization.assess possible impersonation
display name
The sender label shown by a message interface.inspect the display name
sender address
The address presented as the origin of a message.verify the sender address
reply-to address
The destination used when replying to a message.compare the reply-to address
message header
Technical metadata associated with an email.preserve message headers
link destination
The address to which a link points.assess the link destination
attachment
A file included with a message.report an unexpected attachment
credential
Information or a token used to support authentication.protect authentication credentials
credential harvesting
Deceptively collecting authentication information.investigate credential harvesting
MFA prompt
A request for an additional authentication step.report an unexpected MFA prompt
urgency cue
Language pressuring someone to act quickly.identify an urgency cue
out-of-band verification
Checking through a separate trusted communication route.use out-of-band verification
known contact channel
A previously verified way to reach a person or team.use a known contact channel
reporting tool
An approved mechanism for submitting suspicious content.use the reporting tool
reporter
A person who raises a concern or observation.acknowledge the reporter
intake
Initial collection of facts for assessment.complete security intake
interaction history
A factual account of actions taken with content.record interaction history
message classification
An assessed category assigned to a message.confirm message classification
compromise
Loss of the intended security of an account or system.investigate possible compromise
quarantine
Controlled separation of suspicious content or systems.verify quarantine status
reporting delay
Time between a relevant event and its report.measure reporting delay

The four-minute reporting interval is a fact about this case. Thanking the reporter supports the response, but the responder should not promise an outcome that belongs to another process or declare a clean device without assessment.

C Notice the language

Make accurate reporting easier

Thank the person for reporting and ask neutral, concrete questions about actions. Do not promise immunity from every workplace consequence or declare the device safe without evidence. Reassurance should support truthful disclosure and the reporting process, not substitute for assessment.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Welcome the report
"Thank you for reporting it promptly."
Ask neutrally
"What did you do after opening the message?"
Locate the event
"What time did you click the link?"
Separate the actions
"Did you enter credentials, open a file, or approve a sign-in prompt?"
Protect secrets
"Do not tell me your password."
Attribute the account
"You report that no credentials were entered."
Avoid premature reassurance
"We have not established whether there was any compromise."
Stop further interaction
"Do not interact with the message further."
Use the approved route
"Submit the original through the reporting tool."
Preserve relevant details
"Keep the message details available for the response team."
Avoid ordinary forwarding
"Use the approved tool, not a wider distribution list."
Verify independently
"Use a known Payroll contact channel for any business confirmation."
Name the pretext
"The message claims that payroll action is urgent."
Keep classification open
"The message is still under assessment."
Support disclosure
"Accurate details help us choose the right response."
Close with a clear next step
"The response team will assess the report and provide instructions."
6 language and precision notes
Claimed sender
Not yet an authenticated sender.
Clicked
Distinguish from entering credentials or opening a file.
Reported
Attributes the information without overstating verification.
No credentials
A relevant fact, not an all-clear.
Thank you
Encourages reporting without promising immunity or outcomes.
Urgent
A reason to verify, not a reason to bypass the reporting route.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is most useful?

Answer and explanation

B. Thank you for reporting. What actions did you take, and when?

The useful opening encourages disclosure and gathers facts without blame or secret collection.

2. Which status accurately reflects the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. A suspicious-message report is under assessment; compromise is not established.

Neither message classification nor compromise has been established in the supplied case.

3. What should Ben use to submit the original?

Answer and explanation

A. The approved reporting tool

The briefing specifies the approved reporting tool and says to avoid further interaction.

4. Which confirmation route avoids trusting the suspicious message?

Answer and explanation

D. A previously verified Payroll contact channel

An independent known channel does not rely on contact details supplied by the suspect message.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Report the click, without guessing the consequence

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Employee reporter / Leila: Security responder

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • interaction history
  • reporting tool
  • out-of-band verification
  • reporting promptly
  • credential
  • compromise
  • Message classification
  • display name
  • intake
  • reporting delay
  1. Ben
    I clicked a payroll email and now I am worried it was suspicious. I reported it quickly, but I am concerned that I will be blamed.
  2. Leila
    Thank you for 1. Accurate details help the response. Let us go through what you actually did, without guessing the consequence.
  3. Ben
    The email said I needed to confirm payroll information urgently. It showed Payroll as the sender name, so I assumed it came from our team.
  4. Leila
    That 2 is useful context, but it does not verify the sender. Tell me the click time and what appeared next, without interacting with the message again.
  5. Ben
    I clicked at nine thirty-two UTC and reported it at nine thirty-six. A page opened. I did not type anything into it.
  6. Leila
    I will record the 3. Did you enter credentials, open an attachment, or approve a sign-in prompt? Those are separate actions, and we need each one described accurately.
  7. Ben
    No credentials, no attachment, and no prompt approval. I can give you my password if that helps you check whether the account was affected.
  8. Leila
    Please do not share any 4. We do not need your password in this conversation. We need the account identifier and factual actions through the approved response process.
  9. Ben
    I did not enter anything. Can I tell my manager the account is safe, or do you still need to check?
  10. Leila
    We have not established 5, and I cannot give an all-clear from that fact alone. Your report narrows the assessment; it does not replace it.
  11. Ben
    Understood. I still have the original email. Should I forward it to everyone in my department so they can recognize it and avoid clicking?
  12. Leila
    Use the approved 6 for the original. Do not distribute the message more widely or interact further. The response team will assess it and coordinate any necessary communication.
  13. Ben
    I will use that route. The message also gives a phone number for Payroll. Should I call that number to ask whether they sent it?
  14. Leila
    Use 7 through a Payroll channel you already know is genuine. A number supplied only in the suspicious message would rely on the same unverified source.
  15. Ben
    I can reach Payroll through our established internal contact. For the security report, I will keep the times and the original message available.
  16. Leila
    Good. The 8 was four minutes in this case. Prompt reporting gives us useful information sooner, but we still need the assessment before describing the outcome.
  17. Ben
    I almost left out the click. I felt embarrassed and thought the fact that I had not entered a password was the important part.
  18. Leila
    Complete 9 depends on those details. Please keep the account accurate. I can explain our response steps, but I should not make promises about decisions outside my role.
  19. Ben
    My manager can hear that I clicked, reported four minutes later, and did not enter credentials, open an attachment, or approve a prompt. Assessment is pending.
  20. Leila
    Exactly. 10 is still open. Submit the original through the approved tool and follow the response team's instructions. Do not treat either embarrassment or reassurance as evidence.

Answers and explanations
  1. reporting promptly Turn 2. Thanking the employee for reporting promptly encourages factual disclosure without blame or promises about employment outcomes.
  2. display name Turn 4. The display name is the visible sender label, which does not by itself establish that the message is genuine.
  3. interaction history Turn 6. The interaction history distinguishes the click from later actions such as entering data, opening files, or approving prompts.
  4. credential Turn 8. A credential supports authentication and should not be disclosed during this factual intake conversation.
  5. compromise Turn 10. Compromise is not confirmed, but the absence of reported credential entry alone does not prove that no risk exists.
  6. reporting tool Turn 12. The reporting tool is the stated route for submitting the original message without spreading it through an ordinary distribution list.
  7. out-of-band verification Turn 14. Out-of-band verification uses a separate trusted route rather than contact details supplied by the suspicious communication.
  8. reporting delay Turn 16. The reporting delay is four minutes between the supplied click and report times, not a measure of confirmed harm.
  9. intake Turn 18. Intake gathers the relevant facts needed for assessment; omitting the click would distort the response team's understanding.
  10. Message classification Turn 20. Message classification remains pending until assessment determines the appropriate category; a familiar label does not settle it.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: I clicked a payroll email and now I am worried it was suspicious. I reported it quickly, but I am concerned that I will be blamed.
  2. Leila: Thank you for reporting promptly. Accurate details help the response. Let us go through what you actually did, without guessing the consequence.
  3. Ben: The email said I needed to confirm payroll information urgently. It showed Payroll as the sender name, so I assumed it came from our team.
  4. Leila: That display name is useful context, but it does not verify the sender. Tell me the click time and what appeared next, without interacting with the message again.
  5. Ben: I clicked at nine thirty-two UTC and reported it at nine thirty-six. A page opened. I did not type anything into it.
  6. Leila: I will record the interaction history. Did you enter credentials, open an attachment, or approve a sign-in prompt? Those are separate actions, and we need each one described accurately.
  7. Ben: No credentials, no attachment, and no prompt approval. I can give you my password if that helps you check whether the account was affected.
  8. Leila: Please do not share any credential. We do not need your password in this conversation. We need the account identifier and factual actions through the approved response process.
  9. Ben: I did not enter anything. Can I tell my manager the account is safe, or do you still need to check?
  10. Leila: We have not established compromise, and I cannot give an all-clear from that fact alone. Your report narrows the assessment; it does not replace it.
  11. Ben: Understood. I still have the original email. Should I forward it to everyone in my department so they can recognize it and avoid clicking?
  12. Leila: Use the approved reporting tool for the original. Do not distribute the message more widely or interact further. The response team will assess it and coordinate any necessary communication.
  13. Ben: I will use that route. The message also gives a phone number for Payroll. Should I call that number to ask whether they sent it?
  14. Leila: Use out-of-band verification through a Payroll channel you already know is genuine. A number supplied only in the suspicious message would rely on the same unverified source.
  15. Ben: I can reach Payroll through our established internal contact. For the security report, I will keep the times and the original message available.
  16. Leila: Good. The reporting delay was four minutes in this case. Prompt reporting gives us useful information sooner, but we still need the assessment before describing the outcome.
  17. Ben: I almost left out the click. I felt embarrassed and thought the fact that I had not entered a password was the important part.
  18. Leila: Complete intake depends on those details. Please keep the account accurate. I can explain our response steps, but I should not make promises about decisions outside my role.
  19. Ben: My manager can hear that I clicked, reported four minutes later, and did not enter credentials, open an attachment, or approve a prompt. Assessment is pending.
  20. Leila: Exactly. Message classification is still open. Submit the original through the approved tool and follow the response team's instructions. Do not treat either embarrassment or reassurance as evidence.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Thank the reporter without declaring a verdict

Fictional awareness exchange using the lesson case: an employee reports a suspicious message and fears blame; the message is unassessed.

  1. Department coordinator: I reported a suspicious message, but the reply sounded as though I had caused a security incident.
  2. Security awareness lead: I'm sorry the reply implied blame. Reporting a concern is helpful, and this message hasn't been assessed yet.
  3. Department coordinator: So you're not saying that my report proves I fell for phishing or caused damage?
  4. Security awareness lead: Correct. We need the relevant facts through the reporting process before drawing any conclusion.
  5. Department coordinator: What should the corrected response say so colleagues aren't discouraged from speaking up?
  6. Security awareness lead: Thank you for reporting; the team will assess the message and ask for any needed details.
  7. Department coordinator: Should I forward it widely to warn everyone while the assessment is still pending?
  8. Security awareness lead: Please use the established reporting channel rather than spreading the suspicious content unnecessarily.
  9. Department coordinator: I'll keep the report there and describe only what I observed, without guessing its intent.
  10. Security awareness lead: Thank you. A supportive reporting culture depends on useful evidence and clear communication, not premature blame.
A simulation result should not become a public accusation

Fictional awareness program: a draft summary names an employee as careless after one training simulation interaction; the interaction details have not been reviewed.

  1. Training manager: The draft calls one employee careless after a simulation interaction. Is that an appropriate awareness message?
  2. Security educator: No. It assigns a personal trait from limited evidence and could discourage future reporting.
  3. Training manager: I intended to highlight the learning opportunity, but the wording makes it sound like public blame.
  4. Security educator: Let's correct it to describe the observed interaction and the support available, within appropriate privacy boundaries.
  5. Training manager: We haven't reviewed the interaction details yet. Should we say the employee entered credentials?
  6. Security educator: Not without evidence. Keep the behavior unspecified until the authorized reviewer confirms what actually occurred.
  7. Training manager: I'll remove the name from the broad summary and ask the program owner to review the wording.
  8. Security educator: Good. Security awareness should help people recognize social engineering and report concerns without unsupported accusations.
  9. Training manager: The corrected summary will describe the training topic and next learning step, not an individual's character.
  10. Security educator: I'll review it for factual accuracy and a reporting culture that encourages useful questions.
Correcting an all-clear before assessment

Fictional service-desk exchange: a suspicious message was called safe because it displayed a familiar sender name; assessment is still pending.

  1. Office manager: Your first reply said the message was safe because the sender name looked familiar. Is that confirmed?
  2. Security analyst: No. That reassurance was premature, and I apologize. The displayed name alone doesn't establish legitimacy.
  3. Office manager: Then should I tell everyone that it is definitely phishing instead of repeating the all-clear?
  4. Security analyst: Not yet. The assessment is pending, so neither definite label is supported by our current evidence.
  5. Office manager: Please give me a corrected message that doesn't create another unsupported claim for the team.
  6. Security analyst: The earlier all-clear was unverified; the message is being assessed through the security reporting process.
  7. Office manager: Should colleagues keep sending copies to one another to compare what they received?
  8. Security analyst: Ask them to use the established reporting channel and follow the response team's guidance instead.
  9. Office manager: I'll correct the reassurance and thank the colleague who questioned it rather than blame them.
  10. Security analyst: Thank you. I'll provide the assessed outcome when available and keep uncertainty explicit meanwhile.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Report the click, without guessing the consequence
  1. Read turns 1-10. Use a calm tone and distinguish each reported action.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress the trusted reporting route without promising an all-clear.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report actions separately

An employee clicked a suspicious link at 14:10 UTC and reported it at 14:17. They report no credential entry. Message classification is pending. The approved tool is the required reporting route.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Responder: "The reporting interval was ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

D. seven

The interval from 14:10 to 14:17 UTC is seven minutes.

2. Employee: "I report no ___ entry."

Answer and explanation

B. credential

The supplied fact concerns credential entry rather than every possible interaction or risk.

3. Responder: "Message classification is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. pending

The briefing explicitly leaves message classification unresolved at this stage.

4. Employee: "I will use the approved reporting ___."

Answer and explanation

D. tool

The local process specifies the approved tool as the reporting route.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Security Awareness and Phishing
Original fictional case: Employee Ben calls security responder Leila about an email claiming to be from Payroll. He clicked its link at 09:32 UTC and reported it at 09:36. He says he entered no credentials, opened no attachment, and approved no sign-in prompt. He worries that reporting will get him blamed. The message has not been classified, and compromise is not established. The local process routes the original message through the approved reporting tool. Leila asks for factual details, not passwords, and tells Ben not to interact further while the response team assesses it.
Language workshop: Report the click, without guessing the consequence
Communication goal: Elicit a precise suspicious-message report, encourage prompt disclosure, and avoid requests for secrets or unsupported reassurance.
Language guidance: No credentials entered is important reported information, not proof that no risk exists. Preserve what the employee says as a report until corroborated where needed. Message appearance and a familiar display name do not establish authenticity.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Thank you for reporting it promptly." / "What did you do after opening the message?" / "What time did you click the link?" / "Did you enter credentials, open a file, or approve a sign-in prompt?" / "Do not tell me your password." / "You report that no credentials were entered." / "We have not established whether there was any compromise." / "Do not interact with the message further." / "Submit the original through the reporting tool." / "Keep the message details available for the response team." / "Use the approved tool, not a wider distribution list." / "Use a known Payroll contact channel for any business confirmation." / "The message claims that payroll action is urgent." / "The message is still under assessment." / "Accurate details help us choose the right response." / "The response team will assess the report and provide instructions."
Vocabulary: phishing: Deceptive communication intended to induce unsafe disclosure or action. / social engineering: Manipulating people to gain information or access. / pretext: A claimed reason used to justify a request. / impersonation: Pretending to be a trusted person or organization. / display name: The sender label shown by a message interface. / sender address: The address presented as the origin of a message. / reply-to address: The destination used when replying to a message. / message header: Technical metadata associated with an email. / link destination: The address to which a link points. / attachment: A file included with a message. / credential: Information or a token used to support authentication. / credential harvesting: Deceptively collecting authentication information. / MFA prompt: A request for an additional authentication step. / urgency cue: Language pressuring someone to act quickly. / out-of-band verification: Checking through a separate trusted communication route. / known contact channel: A previously verified way to reach a person or team. / reporting tool: An approved mechanism for submitting suspicious content. / reporter: A person who raises a concern or observation. / intake: Initial collection of facts for assessment. / interaction history: A factual account of actions taken with content. / message classification: An assessed category assigned to a message. / compromise: Loss of the intended security of an account or system. / quarantine: Controlled separation of suspicious content or systems. / reporting delay: Time between a relevant event and its report.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Use a calm tone and distinguish each reported action. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress the trusted reporting route without promising an all-clear. Check the transfer. Read the seven-minute interval and pending classification accurately.
Partner: Ben: Employee reporter / Leila: Security responder
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An employee clicked a suspicious link at 14:10 UTC and reported it at 14:17. They report no credential entry. Message classification is pending. The approved tool is the required reporting route.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: I almost left out the click. I felt embarrassed and thought the fact that I had not entered a password was the important part.
Leila: Complete intake depends on those details. Please keep the account accurate. I can explain our response steps, but I should not make promises about decisions outside my role.
Ben: My manager can hear that I clicked, reported four minutes later, and did not enter credentials, open an attachment, or approve a prompt. Assessment is pending.
Leila: Exactly. Message classification is still open. Submit the original through the approved tool and follow the response team's instructions. Do not treat either embarrassment or reassurance as evidence.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
