Dialogue 01: Incident triage
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cybersecurity English
Professional audience: security analysts, SOC staff, incident responders, security engineers, GRC specialists, identity teams, vulnerability managers, and security leaders
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not a security certification, incident-response playbook, legal opinion, or authorization to test systems. Follow the responsible team, approved procedures, evidence-handling rules, and applicable requirements. All organizations, incidents, figures, priorities, and control criteria in the cases are invented.
Dialogue 01: Incident triage
Original fictional setting: Security reviews an unusual sign-in alert.
Professional roles: SOC analyst / Incident lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
SOC analyst: The alert shows an unfamiliar location and repeated failed sign-ins. Compromise isn't confirmed.
Incident lead: What do the successful sign-ins and device records show?
SOC analyst: One success from an unmanaged device. I'm preserving the logs under our incident process.
Incident lead: Correlate the activity and follow the approved containment and escalation playbook.
SOC analyst: I'll document the evidence, uncertainty, and affected account without sharing credentials.
Incident lead: I'll assign incident coordination and confirm when stakeholders should receive the next factual update.
Vocabulary in this exchange: alert: A notification that an event or condition may need attention. / incident: Unplanned event that disrupts service, safety, quality, security, operations, or expected performance. / containment: Action intended to limit the spread or effect of a problem while it is addressed.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cybersecurity English
Professional audience: security analysts, SOC staff, incident responders, security engineers, GRC specialists, identity teams, vulnerability managers, and security leaders
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not a security certification, incident-response playbook, legal opinion, or authorization to test systems. Follow the responsible team, approved procedures, evidence-handling rules, and applicable requirements. All organizations, incidents, figures, priorities, and control criteria in the cases are invented.
Dialogue 01: Incident triage
Original fictional setting: Security reviews an unusual sign-in alert.
Professional roles: SOC analyst / Incident lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
SOC analyst: The alert shows an unfamiliar location and repeated failed sign-ins. Compromise isn't confirmed.
Incident lead: What do the successful sign-ins and device records show?
SOC analyst: One success from an unmanaged device. I'm preserving the logs under our incident process.
Incident lead: Correlate the activity and follow the approved containment and escalation playbook.
SOC analyst: I'll document the evidence, uncertainty, and affected account without sharing credentials.
Incident lead: I'll assign incident coordination and confirm when stakeholders should receive the next factual update.
Vocabulary in this exchange: alert: A notification that an event or condition may need attention. / incident: Unplanned event that disrupts service, safety, quality, security, operations, or expected performance. / containment: Action intended to limit the spread or effect of a problem while it is addressed.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 02: Vulnerability priority
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Course: Cybersecurity English

Dialogue 02: Vulnerability priority
Original fictional setting: Security and operations review a critical-severity finding.
Professional roles: Vulnerability analyst / Service owner
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Vulnerability analyst: The scanner rated this critical. Is the affected service externally reachable?
Service owner: The asset inventory says internal, but the exposure mapping needs confirmation.
Vulnerability analyst: Severity is one input. We also need exploitability, business impact, and compensating controls.
Service owner: I'll verify the actual deployment and patch compatibility with the platform team.
Vulnerability analyst: I'll check the advisory and our approved remediation requirements.
Service owner: Then we'll agree on an owner and schedule, escalating any exception through the risk process.
Vocabulary in this exchange: exploitability: How feasible it is for an attacker to take advantage of a vulnerability. / remediation: Work to correct a problem or reduce its effects; in environmental work, addressing contamination.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 03: Privileged access review
Original fictional setting: An application team requests permanent administrator access.
Professional roles: Application lead / Identity engineer
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Application lead: Our support team wants standing administrator rights to reduce ticket delays.
Identity engineer: Which tasks require elevated privileges, and how often?
Application lead: Mostly configuration changes during escalations. Routine support doesn't need them.
Identity engineer: Then let's assess time-limited access with approval and logging for those specific tasks.
Application lead: That could work if the request process fits our support hours.
Identity engineer: I'll map the workflow with you and test it before we propose a controlled access change.
END REFERENCE

Course: Cybersecurity English

Dialogue 04: Threat-model workshop
Original fictional setting: A team reviews a new external integration.
Professional roles: Security architect / Software engineer
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Security architect: Where does untrusted input cross into the service, and what authority does that request carry?
Software engineer: At the partner endpoint. The service then calls our account system with its own identity.
Security architect: That trust boundary deserves attention. How do we restrict what the partner can request?
Software engineer: The design includes scoped operations, but the authorization checks need review.
Security architect: Let's document abuse cases and verify controls against them.
Software engineer: I'll update the data-flow diagram and link the open security questions to the design review.
Vocabulary in this exchange: trust boundary: A point where data or activity crosses between areas with different trust or access assumptions.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 05: A risk exception
Original fictional setting: A project asks to delay a required security control.
Professional roles: Project manager / Security risk lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Project manager: The release date is fixed. Can you approve skipping the control for a month?
Security risk lead: I can assess the request, but risk acceptance belongs to the designated authority.
Project manager: What should the exception request contain?
Security risk lead: Scope, exposure, reason, compensating controls, owner, expiry, and the remediation plan.
Project manager: I'll assemble that and identify what changes if approval is refused.
Security risk lead: We'll record the decision explicitly. A submitted request doesn't authorize the exception.
Vocabulary in this exchange: control: A process, approval, check, or technical safeguard designed to reduce risk. / risk acceptance: An authorized decision to retain a stated risk under specified conditions. / remediation: Work to correct a problem or reduce its effects; in environmental work, addressing contamination.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Dialogue 06: Audit evidence
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 06: Audit evidence
Original fictional setting: A control owner prepares access-review evidence.
Professional roles: Control owner / Security auditor
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Control owner: Here's a screenshot showing the review dashboard is green.
Security auditor: I also need evidence of the review period, population, reviewer, decisions, and follow-up.
Control owner: The underlying export includes accounts that were disabled after the review.
Security auditor: Keep the timing clear. We need to distinguish review results from later remediation.
Control owner: I'll provide the approved records through the controlled evidence channel.
Security auditor: I'll assess them against the control requirement and identify any remaining evidence gaps.
Vocabulary in this exchange: control: A process, approval, check, or technical safeguard designed to reduce risk. / remediation: Work to correct a problem or reduce its effects; in environmental work, addressing contamination.

Dialogue 07: Executive incident update

Chief operating officer: What decisions do you need from me now?
Security lead: Confirm the business contact for continuity coordination; communications and legal are reviewing external obligations.
Chief operating officer: I'll assign that contact. Keep the next update clear about confirmed facts, open questions, and operational effects.

Ready to paste: the complete prompt is already written for you, including the selected lesson or dialogue. Choose an activity and copy it; no prompt writing or assembly is required.

You take one role. The AI answers as the other professional.

These settings adjust the practice; they are not an assessment of your level.

Lesson 01: Security Triage and Alert Investigation
Original fictional case: Analyst Anika reviews alert S14 with shift lead Luis. The identity log records a successful privileged-account sign-in at 02:14 UTC from an unfamiliar address. A maintenance ticket covers 02:00-03:00 UTC, but its host and account have not been matched to the event. The account owner has not replied. Application logs are missing for 02:10-02:20 UTC. No malicious action is confirmed. The local process requires escalation to Luis now while correlation continues.
Language workshop: A successful login is not verified authorization
Communication goal: Give a concise alert handoff that separates logged events, plausible explanations, and unresolved authorization.
Language guidance: A successful authentication does not establish that the account holder intended or authorized the action. A matching time window supports a possible maintenance explanation, not a confirmed match to this event.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The identity log records a successful sign-in at 02:14 UTC." / "The event involves a privileged account." / "Authorization is not yet verified." / "The timing overlaps planned maintenance." / "We have not matched the host and account." / "Application logs are missing for ten minutes." / "Who can confirm the authorized operator?" / "I am escalating while correlation continues." / "No malicious action is confirmed at this point." / "That conclusion applies only to the sources checked." / "The address is unfamiliar, not yet classified as hostile." / "Please provide the relevant maintenance approval." / "Both timestamps are reported in UTC." / "The maintenance window alone does not close the alert." / "We will compare the ticket, account, and host." / "S14 remains under investigation with a documented visibility gap."
Vocabulary: alert: A notification that a detection condition was met. / event: An observable occurrence recorded by a system. / incident: An occurrence assessed as compromising or threatening security. / triage: Initial assessment to decide priority and next handling. / privileged account: An identity with elevated system permissions. / successful sign-in: A recorded completion of an authentication process. / authentication: Checking evidence that an identity claim is valid. / authorization: Permission for a particular action or resource. / source address: A network address associated with an observed connection. / timestamp: A recorded time associated with an event. / UTC: Coordinated Universal Time, a shared time reference. / maintenance window: A defined period for planned maintenance work. / correlation: Relating records to assess whether events are connected. / log source: A system or service producing event records. / telemetry: System observations collected for monitoring or analysis. / visibility gap: A period or area lacking needed observation data. / baseline: A reference pattern used to evaluate current behavior. / anomaly: A departure from an expected pattern. / false positive: A detection that incorrectly indicates the target condition. / benign activity: Activity assessed as not harmful in its context. / indicator: An observable sign that can support an investigation. / hypothesis: A proposed explanation awaiting evaluation. / confidence level: A qualified assessment of evidential strength. / case record: A documented account of observations and handling.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress observed facts separately from the maintenance hypothesis. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep authentication distinct from authorization. Check the transfer. Read the UTC time and the two missing-evidence statements clearly.
Partner: Anika: Security analyst / Luis: Shift lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Alert T8 records a sign-in at 05:20 UTC. Maintenance was scheduled from 05:00-06:00, but the account match is unverified. Application logs are unavailable. The shift lead has received the escalation.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Anika: I will compare the ticket against the host and account, then update the working explanation when that evidence arrives. No malicious action is confirmed.
Luis: Describe your confidence level carefully. We have a real observation and unresolved context. Repeating the same possibility does not turn it into stronger evidence.
Anika: My handoff will keep the event time in UTC and state that the missing application records limit our ability to exclude later activity.
Luis: Good. Continue the correlation and keep me involved under the escalation process. The status is under investigation, with authorization unverified and the visibility limitation clearly recorded.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Paste into a new chat, then follow the exercise one step at a time. English Ladder does not send anything to an AI service. Your chosen service's terms and any usage charges apply.

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.

Use the learner book for extended dialogue practice, vocabulary and collocations, reusable phrases, and explained answer keys.

Language notes and further reading

The cases and explanations are original teaching material. The references provide language frameworks and selected professional context; use current local guidance for actual work.

  • NIST. SP 800-61 Revision 3: Incident Response Recommendations and Considerations, April 2025.
  • CISA. Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog.
  • OWASP. Threat Modeling Cheat Sheet.
  • NIST. SP 800-53 Revision 5: Security and Privacy Controls for Information Systems and Organizations.
